bound. In noise injection, for example, we can decompose the augmented matrix into a matrix of identical rows and the independent Gaussian noise. This latter matrix is asymptotically free from the first one, and thus we can obtain control on its smallest singular value. To extend this into a proof covering all DA schemes will be left to future work, and for now we will work under the mild constraint that
\hat{\beta}\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
.

D.2
Generalized Theorem

In this section, we will prove (
5
) from
2
. However, we actually prove a slightly more general result:

Theorem 15
.

Let
\left(X_{i},y_{i}(X_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
and
\left(G_{i},y_{i}(G_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
be generated under Assumptions
1
,
3
-
5
, and
12
, where each
G_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\text{\rm Var}(X_{i})\right)
. Then for any
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{p}
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)\to 0.

We remark that if we successfully establish
15
, then the claim in (
5
) of
2
directly follows by setting
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{S}_{p}
.

D.3
Converting the Loss

Before continuing, we will convert our labels from
\{0,1\}
to
\{-1,1\}
, as this combines two of the terms in the training risk to significantly simplify calculations. To be specific, noting that
y_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, we can define
\tilde{y}_{i}:=2y_{i}-1\in\{-1,1\}
, which still satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{y}_{i}=1\mid X_{i}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(y_{i}=1\mid X_{i}\right)=\sigma(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}).

Then the loss evaluated at each data point
(X_{i},y_{i})
can be re-expressed as

\displaystyle\log\big( 1 + e^{ X_i^\intercal\beta} \big)-y_{i}X_{i}^{\intercal}\beta\;=

\displaystyle\;\Big(\log\big( 1 + e^{ X_i^\intercal\beta} \big)-X_{i}^{\intercal}\beta\Big)\,\mathbb{I}\{y_{i}=1\}+\log\big( 1 + e^{ X_i^\intercal\beta} \big)\,\mathbb{I}\{y_{i}=0\}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\log\Big( \mfrac{1 + e^{X_i^\intercal\beta}}{ e^{X_i^\intercal\beta}} \Big)\,\mathbb{I}\{y_{i}=1\}+\log\big( 1 + e^{ X_i^\intercal\beta} \big)\,\mathbb{I}\{y_{i}=0\}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\log\big( 1 + e^{- X_i^\intercal\beta} \big)\,\mathbb{I}\{\tilde{y}_{i}=1\}+\log\big( 1 + e^{ X_i^\intercal\beta} \big)\,\mathbb{I}\{\tilde{y}_{i}=-1\}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\log\big( 1 + e^{- \tilde y_i X_i^\intercal\beta} \big)\;.

Thus, renaming our labels as
y_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
, for the rest of this section and also
Appendix

H
, we use the training risk

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{n}(\beta,\mathbf{X})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\log(1 + e^{-y_iX_i^\intercal\beta})+\frac{\lambda}{2n}\|\beta\|^{2}.

D.4
Definitions

To complete the proof, we must first introduce the various terminology and techniques that are used throughout, from smoothing the labels and minimum function to the continuous Lindeberg interpolation.

D.4.1
Smoothing the Labels

First we will define the way in which we smooth our labels and subsequently the risk function. To do so, let us define the mollifier
\zeta_{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for
\gamma\in(0,1)
as

\displaystyle\zeta_{\gamma}(x):=\textsf{C}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{x^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}(|x|<\gamma),

where
C
is chosen such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta_{\gamma}(x)\,\text{d}x=1
. Then for a given function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
we define

\displaystyle f_{\gamma}:=f\ast\zeta_{\gamma}

as the convolution of
f
with
\zeta_{\gamma}
, noting that this makes
f
smooth. We can then define a smoothed version of the labels as

\displaystyle\eta_{i}:=\bm{1}^{\pm}_{\gamma}\left(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i}\right).

(14)

From here we can define the new smoothed risk as

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\log\left(1+e^{-\eta_{i}X_{i}^{\intercal}\beta}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{2n}\|\beta\|_{2}^{2},

where we have replaced each
y_{i}
with its smoothed counterpart
\eta_{i}
.

D.4.2
Smoothing the Minimum

Next we will define the function that will be used to approximate the minimum over our parameter space. For the set
\mathcal{\tilde{S}}
, define the smoothed minimum

\displaystyle f_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}^{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}\to\mathbb{R},\ \ \ f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{X}):=\frac{-1}{n\alpha}\log\left(\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}\exp\left[-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X})\right]\right),

where the sum is over the minimal
\delta\sqrt{p}
-net
\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}
.
When
\alpha
is fixed or understood from context, we will refer to
f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{X})
as simply
f_{\delta}(\mathbf{X})
.

D.4.3
Interpolation Technique

Finally, we define the interpolation that we will use for the proof of our main theorem. For
t\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2}]
, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{t}:=\sin(t)\mathbf{X}+\cos(t)\mathbf{G}.

When
t
is fixed or understood from context, we will refer to
\mathbf{U}^{t}
as simply
\mathbf{U}
. Now, for each
i=1,\ldots,n
, define the weight functions

\displaystyle w_{\gamma}(\beta):=\frac{e^{-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta,\mathbf{U})}}{\sum_{\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}e^{-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta^{\prime},\mathbf{U})}},\quad\quad\quad w_{\gamma}^{i,k}(\beta):=\frac{e^{-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma,i,k}(\beta,\mathbf{U})}}{\sum_{\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}e^{-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma,i,k}(\beta^{\prime},\mathbf{U})}}.

Also define expectation with respect to the density induced by these weights as

\displaystyle\langle\textsf{g}(\beta)\rangle:=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}\hskip-5.0ptw_{\gamma}(\beta)\hskip 1.0pt\textsf{g}(\beta),\quad\quad\quad\langle\textsf{g}(\beta)\rangle_{i,k}:=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}\hskip-5.0ptw_{\gamma}^{i,k}(\beta)\hskip 1.0pt\textsf{g}(\beta),

(15)

where

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma,i,k}(\beta;\bm{\eta},\mathbf{U}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\notin\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\log\left(1+e^{-\eta_{j}U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{2n}\|\beta\|_{2}^{2}

represents the risk taken only over the points outside the block
\mathcal{B}_{i}
containing
X_{i}
. Also for each
i=1,\ldots,n
define the conditional expectation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}[\ \cdot\ ]:=\mathbb{E}[\ \cdot\ \mid\mathbf{U}^{ik}],

where
\mathbf{U}^{ik}
is used to denote the interpolation matrix without
\mathcal{B}_{i}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{ik}:=\left(U_{1},\ldots,U_{i-1},0,\ldots,0,U_{i+k},\ldots,U_{n}\right),

noting that this forces
\mathbf{U}^{ik}\indep U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}
. Lastly, we also define a “gradient” term

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta):=\left(\eta_{i}\omega_{i}\sigma_{i\beta}\right)\beta+\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\sigma_{j\beta}\eta_{j}^{\prime}a_{ji}U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta\right)\beta^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},

where
\sigma_{i\beta}:=\sigma(-\eta_{i}{U_{i}}^{\intercal}\beta)
and
\eta_{i}^{\prime}:={\bm{1}^{\pm}_{\gamma}}^{\prime}(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}{U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i})
. When the data matrix is clear from context, we will write
\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta)
as simply
\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)
. Lastly, we use the shorthand

\displaystyle\ell(a,b):=\log(1+e^{-ab}),\quad\quad\ell_{i}(\beta):=\ell(\eta_{i},U_{i}^{\intercal}\beta).

With these, we are ready to begin the proof.

D.5
Proof of the Theorem
15

In this subsection we finally prove
15
which, from our previous remarks, immediately proves (
5
) of
2
.

Proof of
15

For ease, let us refer to the quantity of interest as

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle=(\star).

Let
\alpha,\delta,\gamma,\tau>0
. We may first bound

\displaystyle(\star)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X})\right)+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

(16)

\displaystyle+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}2\textsf{C}_{1}\sqrt{k\gamma}+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right),

(17)

where
(i)
follows from applying
22
to the first and third summands. Then, we use
23
to bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)\leq d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{X}),f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{G})\right)+\textsf{C}_{2}\left(\sqrt{k}\delta+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

(18)

From here, we will prove universality for
f_{\delta}(\mathbf{X})
, and then show why this is sufficient. Recall from above the interpolator

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{t}:=\sin(t)\mathbf{X}+\cos(t)\mathbf{G},\ \ \ t\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2}].

By the fundamental theorem of calculus, since
\mathbf{U}^{0}=\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{U}^{\pi/2}=\mathbf{X}
, we may bound

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\E\l[h\l(f_\delta(\bX)\r) - h\l(f_\delta(\bG)\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\partial_t h\l(f_\delta(\bU^t)\r)\r]}\,\text{d}t.

Using the chain rule we may expand

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)=\frac{-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\rangle

where we set

\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{t}:=\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}^{t}=\cos(t)\mathbf{X}-\sin(t)\mathbf{G}.

Using
24
we obtain that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\absolutevalue{\E\l[h\l(f_\delta(\bX)\r) - h\l(f_\delta(\bG)\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\partial_t h\l(f_\delta(\bU^t)\r)\r]}\,\text{d}t\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\pi}{2}\tau,

(19)

where
(i)
is from the Dominated Convergence Theorem with the dominating function given by the bound on this derivative in
Lemma

26
, and
(ii)
is from
24
. Combining (
19
) with (
17
) and (
18
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(\star)\leq\textsf{C}\left[\sqrt{k\gamma}+\sqrt{k}\delta+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)+\tau\right].

Noting that the left-hand side is independent of our four parameters
(\alpha,\delta,\gamma,\tau)
, we can take our limits in the proper order to conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(\star)\leq\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau,\gamma\to 0\\
\alpha\to\infty\end{subarray}}\textsf{C}\left[\sqrt{k\gamma}+\sqrt{k}\delta+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)+\tau\right]=0.

\jmlrQED

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
3
(
7
) under Assumption
8
(i)
: Training risk universality under
m
-dependence

The next result restates Theorem
3
(
7
) under Assumption
8
(i)
, i.e. the universality universality of the training risk in the
m
-dependent setting:

Theorem 16

(Training risk universality under
m
-dependence)
.

Let
\left(X_{i},y_{i}(X_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
and
\left(G_{i},y_{i}(G_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
be generated under Assumptions
2
-
4
,
8
(i), and
9
, where each
G_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{0},\text{\rm Var}(X_{i})\right)
. Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)\to 0.

(20)

Proof of
Theorem

16

Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be fixed. Define new matrices
\mathbf{X}^{M},\mathbf{G}^{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}\times p}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{M}

\displaystyle:=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{M},X_{M+m+1},\ldots,X_{2M+m},X_{2M+2m+1},\ldots)^{\intercal}

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{M}

\displaystyle:=(G_{1},\ldots,G_{M},G_{M+m+1},\ldots,G_{2M+m},G_{2M+2m+1},\ldots)^{\intercal},

noting that

\displaystyle n^{\prime}\in[n-(r+1)m+1,\ n-rm]\subset\left[n\frac{M}{M+m}-m,\ n\frac{M}{M+m}+m\right]=[nq-m,nq+m],

(21)

where
r:=\lfloor\frac{n}{M+m}\rfloor
and
q:=\frac{M}{M+m}
. By construction,
\mathbf{X}^{M}
and
\mathbf{G}^{M}
are block dependent with block size
M
. We may also define

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{m}

\displaystyle:=(X_{M+1},\dots,X_{M+m},X_{2M+m+1},\dots,X_{2M+2m},\dots)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{m}

\displaystyle:=(G_{M+1},\dots,G_{M+m},G_{2M+m+1},\dots,G_{2M+2m},\dots)

so that every vector
X_{i}
is either in
\mathbf{X}^{M}
or
\mathbf{X}^{m}
. For simplicity we can also write these indexing sets as

\displaystyle B_{M}

\displaystyle:=\{1,\ldots,M,M+m+1,\ldots,2M+m,\ldots,\}

\displaystyle B_{m}

\displaystyle:=[n]\setminus B_{M}.

If we once again refer to our quantity of interest as

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)=(\star),

then we may bound

\displaystyle(\star)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M})\right)}_{(a)}+\underbrace{d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{G}^{M})\right)}_{(b)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{G}^{M}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)}_{(c)}.

By
2
, we know that
(b)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (\star)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (a)+\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (c).

We may now apply
29
with
\tilde{m}=m
to say that for some
\textsf{C}_{d}>0
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (\star)\leq 2\textsf{C}_{d}\frac{m}{M}\sqrt{M+m}=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{-1/2}\right).

(22)

As this holds for every
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we take
M\to\infty
to obtain the result.

\jmlrQED

Appendix F
Proof of Theorem
3
(
7
) under Assumption
8
(ii)
: Training risk universality under mixing

We first prove the universality of the train risk if the data is made of blocks of size
k
that are almost independent. We will then combine this result with
Theorem

29
to get the desired result.

Assumption 13

(
\beta_{\rm{mix}}
almost independent blocks of size
k
)
.

For all
i,j
such that
j\geq i+k
we have that if we define
\mathcal{A}:=\sigma\big((X_{\ell},y_{\ell})_{\ell\leq i}\big)
and
\mathcal{B}:=\sigma\big((X_{\ell},y_{\ell})_{\ell>j}\big)
then
\beta\Big(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\Big)\leq\beta_{\rm{mix}}
, where
\beta\Big(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\Big)
is the
\beta-
mixing coefficient between the sigma-algebras
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
(see
Bradley (2005)
for a definition).

Under this assumption we can establish the universality of the train risk.

Lemma 17
.

Assume that
(\mathbf{X}_{i},y_{i})
and
(\mathbf{G}_{i},y_{i}(\mathbf{G}_{i}))
satisfy Assumption
2
-
5
.
Assume that
\rm{Var}((\mathbf{G}_{i}))=\rm{Var}((\mathbf{X}_{i})).
Moreover assume that
Assumption

13
also holds. Then we have that there is a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\beta_{\rm mix}^{1/15}

Proof
For ease, let us refer to the quantity of interest as

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle=(\star).

Let
\alpha,\delta,\gamma,\tau>0
. We may first bound

\displaystyle(\star)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X})\right)+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}2\textsf{C}_{1}\sqrt{\gamma}\frac{\sqrt{\max(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2},\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2})}}{\sqrt{n}}+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right),

(23)

where
(i)
follows from applying
22
to the first and third summands. Then, we use
23
to bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

(24)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{X}),f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{G})\right)+\textsf{C}_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\max(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{B}(0,\sqrt{p})}^{2},\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{\mathcal{B}(0,\sqrt{p})}^{2})}}{\sqrt{n}}\delta+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

(25)

Using the bound in
20
under
Assumption

8
(ii) we know that there exists a constant
{\textsf{C}}_{3}>0
such that

\frac{\sqrt{\max(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2},\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2})}}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{\sqrt{\max(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{B}(0,\sqrt{p})}^{2},\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{\mathcal{B}(0,\sqrt{p})}^{2})}}{\sqrt{n}}\leq{\textsf{C}}_{3}\mathcal{S}.

Hence we have

\displaystyle(\star)\leq d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{X}),f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{G})\right)+\textsf{C}_{2}\left({\textsf{C}}_{3}\mathcal{S}\delta+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)+2\textsf{C}_{1}\textsf{C}_{3}\sqrt{\gamma}\mathcal{S}.

(26)

From here, we will prove universality for
f_{\delta}(\mathbf{X})
, and then show why this is sufficient. Recall from above the interpolator

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{t}:=\sin(t)\mathbf{X}+\cos(t)\mathbf{G},\ \ \ t\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2}].

By the fundamental theorem of calculus, since
\mathbf{U}^{0}=\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{U}^{\pi/2}=\mathbf{X}
, we may bound

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\E\l[h\l(f_\delta(\bX)\r) - h\l(f_\delta(\bG)\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\partial_t h\l(f_\delta(\bU^t)\r)\r]}\,\text{d}t.

Using the chain rule we may expand

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)=\frac{-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\rangle

To further bound this, we will replace specific blocks
(X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}})
with independent blocks, allowing us to use the results established in
Section

H.3
. More precisely, we will show that for an average
i\leq n
the expectation
\mathbb{E}\Big[h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\rangle\Big]
is approximately the same if the block containing the i-th observation
\Big(X_{j},y_{j}(X_{j})\Big)_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
is independent from the others. We also use the notation
j>\mathcal{B}_{i}
to denote the set of indices after the last index of the block
\mathcal{B}_{i}
, and analogously use
j<\mathcal{B}_{i}
for those before
\mathcal{B}_{i}
.

In this goal, for
i\leq n
we write
(W_{j}^{i})
the process such that
\Big(W_{j}^{i},y_{j}(W_{j}^{i})\Big)_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
,
\Big(W_{j}^{i},y_{j}(W_{j}^{i})\Big)_{j>\mathcal{B}_{i}}
and
\Big(W_{j}^{i},y_{j}(W_{j}^{i})\Big)_{j<\mathcal{B}_{i}}
are independent and have the same marginals as
\Big(X_{j},y_{j}(X_{j})\Big)_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
,
\Big(X_{j},y_{j}(X_{j})\Big)_{j>\mathcal{B}_{i}}
and
\Big(X_{j},y_{j}(X_{j})\Big)_{j<\mathcal{B}_{i}}
. We define similarly
\Big(G^{i}_{j},y_{j}(G^{i}_{j})\Big)_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
,
\Big(G^{i}_{j},y_{j}(G^{i}_{j})\Big)_{j>\mathcal{B}_{i}}
and
\Big(G^{i}_{j},y_{j}(G^{i}_{j})\Big)_{j<\mathcal{B}_{i}}
. The interpolated process between
W^{i}
and
\mathbf{G}^{i}
is written as

{U}^{(i)}_{j}:=\rm{sin}(t)W_{j}^{i}+\rm{cos}(t)G^{i}_{j},\qquad\tilde{U}^{(i)}_{j}:=\rm{cos}(t)W_{j}^{i}-\rm{sin}(t)G^{i}_{j}.

Denote
\mathcal{D}^{(i)}_{j}(\beta)
the version of
\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)
for
\mathbf{U}^{(i)}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{(i)}
. Similarly we write
\langle\cdot\rangle_{(i)}
the version of
\langle\cdot\rangle
for
U^{(i)}
(see (
15
) for the definition of
\langle\cdot\rangle
).

Choose
L>0
to be a real. Define

\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta):=\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)~\mathbb{I}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|,|U_{i}^{T}\beta|\leq L)

and

\mathcal{D}^{(i)}_{i,L}(\beta):=\mathcal{D}^{(i)}_{i}(\beta)\mathbb{I}(|\tilde{(}U^{(i)}_{i})^{T}\beta|,|(U^{(i)}_{i})^{T}\beta|\leq L).

We first remark that we can switch
\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)
for the truncated
\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)
without changing too much the value of the expectation
\mathbb{E}\Big({-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\rangle\Big)
. Indeed we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big({-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)\rangle\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big({-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\rangle\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big(\langle|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)|\mathbb{I}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|>L)\rangle\Big)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big(\langle|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)|\mathbb{I}(|U_{i}^{T}\beta|>L)\rangle\Big)\

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big(\langle\Big\{|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|+|U_{i}^{\intercal}\beta||\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}|\Big\}~\mathbb{I}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|>L)\rangle\Big)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big(\langle\Big\{|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|+|U_{i}^{\intercal}\beta||\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}|\Big\}~\mathbb{I}(|U_{i}^{T}\beta|>L)\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\frac{1}{nL^{1/4}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big(\langle\Big\{|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|^{5/4}+|U_{i}^{\intercal}\beta||\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|^{1/4}|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}|\Big\}\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{nL^{1/4}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big(\langle\Big\{|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|{|U_{i}^{T}\beta|^{1/4}}+|U_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|^{5/4}|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}|\Big\}\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\frac{1}{nL^{1/4}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big(\langle|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|^{5/4}\rangle\Big)+L^{-1/4}\max_{i\leq n}\|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta\|_{6}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\langle(U_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2}\rangle)}\Big\{{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\langle(U_{i}^{T}\beta)^{3/4}\rangle)}\Big\}^{3/4}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{L^{1/4}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\langle(\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2}\rangle)}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\langle|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|^{3/4}\rangle^{2/3})}+\max_{i}\|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta^{*}\|_{3}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\langle(U_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2}\rangle)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L^{1/4}}\Big\{\mathbb{E}\Big(n^{-1}\|\tilde{U}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}\Big)^{5/8}+\max_{i\leq n}\|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta\|_{6}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Big(n^{-1}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}\Big)}\mathbb{E}\Big(n^{-1}\|\tilde{U}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}\Big)^{9/32}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Big(n^{-1}\|\tilde{U}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}\Big)}\Big({\mathbb{E}\Big(n^{-1}\|U\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}\Big)}\Big)^{1/8}+\max_{i}\|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta^{*}\|_{3}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Big(n^{-1}\|U\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}\Big)}\Big\}.

where (i) is a consequence of the fact that
\mathbb{I}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|\geq L)\leq\frac{|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|^{1/4}}{L^{1/4}}
, (ii) comes from Jensen inequality combined with Hölder inequality. Hence using the bound in
20
under
Assumption

8
(ii) and using
Assumption

3
we have that there exists a constant
\textsf{C}_{4}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big({-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)\rangle\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big({-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\rangle\Big)\Big|\leq\textsf{C}_{4}L^{-1/4}.

(27)

Moreover according to
38
we have that there is a constant
\textsf{C}_{5}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big({-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)\rangle\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big({-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}^{(i)})\right)}\langle({\tilde{\mathbf{U}}}^{(i)})^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}^{(i)}_{i,L}(\beta)\rangle_{(i)}\Big)\Big|

(28)

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}4({3\beta_{\rm{mix}}})^{1/3}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\{L(1+\|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta^{*}\|_{3/2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}L\textsf{C}_{5}({\beta_{\rm{mix}}})^{1/3}

where (i) is due to the fact that
|\tilde{U}_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)|\leq|\tilde{U}_{i}\beta|~\mathbb{I}(|\tilde{U}_{i}\beta|\leq L)+\mathbb{I}(|U_{i}\beta|\leq L)|~\tilde{U}_{i}\beta^{*}||U_{i}^{T}\beta|
combined with the triangle inequality (ii) is due to

assumption

3
which imply that
\limsup_{n}\sup_{i}\|U_{i}^{T}\beta^{*}\|_{3/2}<\infty
. Hence combining
Lemma

24
with
Eqs.

28
and
27
we obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big(\frac{-h^{\prime}\left(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right)}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\rangle\Big)\Big|\leq L\textsf{C}_{5}\beta_{\rm mix}^{1/3}+\tau+\textsf{C}_{4}L^{-1/4}.

(29)

This directly implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\absolutevalue{\E\l[h\l(f_\delta(\bX)\r) - h\l(f_\delta(\bG)\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\partial_t h\l(f_\delta(\bU^t)\r)\r]}\,\text{d}t

(30)

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\pi}{2}\Big[L\textsf{C}_{5}\beta_{\rm mix}^{1/3}+\tau+\textsf{C}_{4}L^{-1/4}\Big]

(31)

where
(i)
is from the Dominated Convergence Theorem with the dominating function given by the bound on this derivative in
Lemma

26
, and
(ii)
is from
Eq.

29
. Combining (
31
) with (
23
) and (
26
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(\star)\leq\textsf{C}\left[\sqrt{k\gamma}+\sqrt{k}\delta+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)+\tau+L\beta_{\rm mix}^{1/3}+L^{-1/4}\right].

Noting that the left-hand side is independent of our four parameters
(\alpha,\delta,\gamma,\tau)
, we can take our limits in the proper order to conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(\star)\leq\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau,\gamma\to 0\\
\alpha\to\infty\end{subarray}}\textsf{C}\left[\sqrt{k\gamma}+\sqrt{k}\delta+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)+\tau\right]=C\left[L\beta_{\rm mix}^{1/3}+L^{-1/4}\right].

Optimizing over
L\geq 0
gives us the desired result.

\jmlrQED

The next result restates Theorem
3
(
7
) under Assumption
8
(ii)
, i.e. training risk universality under mixing:

Theorem 18

(Universality of the training risk under Assumption
8
(ii))
.

Let
\left(X_{i},y_{i}(X_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
and

\left(G_{i},y_{i}(G_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
be generated under Assumptions
2
-
4
,
8
(ii), and
9
, where each
G_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{0},\text{\rm Var}(X_{i})\right)
. Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)\to 0.

(32)

Proof of
Theorem

18

Let
M,\tilde{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be fixed. Define new matrices
\mathbf{X}^{M},\mathbf{G}^{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}\times p}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{M}

\displaystyle:=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{M},X_{M+\tilde{m}+1},\ldots,X_{2M+\tilde{m}},X_{2M+2\tilde{m}+1},\ldots)^{\intercal}

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{M}

\displaystyle:=(G_{1},\ldots,G_{M},G_{M+\tilde{m}+1},\ldots,G_{2M+\tilde{m}},G_{2M+2\tilde{m}+1},\ldots)^{\intercal},

noting that

\displaystyle n^{\prime}\in[n-(r+1)\tilde{m}+1,\ n-r\tilde{m}]\subset\left[n\frac{M}{M+\tilde{m}}-\tilde{m},\ n\frac{M}{M+\tilde{m}}+\tilde{m}\right]=[nq-\tilde{m},nq+\tilde{m}],

(33)

where
r:=\lfloor\frac{n}{M+\tilde{m}}\rfloor
and
q:=\frac{M}{M+\tilde{m}}
. By construction,
\mathbf{X}^{M}
and
\mathbf{G}^{M}
satisfy Assumption
13
with
\beta_{\rm mix}=\beta(\tilde{m})
. We may also define

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\tilde{m}}

\displaystyle:=(X_{M+1},\dots,X_{M+\tilde{m}},X_{2M+\tilde{m}+1},\dots,X_{2M+2\tilde{m}},\dots)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{\tilde{m}}

\displaystyle:=(G_{M+1},\dots,G_{M+\tilde{m}},G_{2M+\tilde{m}+1},\dots,G_{2M+2\tilde{m}},\dots)

so that every vector
X_{i}
is either in
\mathbf{X}^{M}
or
\mathbf{X}^{\tilde{m}}
. For simplicity we can also write these indexing sets as

\displaystyle B_{M}

\displaystyle:=\{1,\ldots,M,M+{\tilde{m}}+1,\ldots,2M+{\tilde{m}},\ldots,\}

\displaystyle B_{\tilde{m}}

\displaystyle:=[n]\setminus B_{M}.

If we once again refer to our quantity of interest as

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)=(\star),

then we may bound

\displaystyle(\star)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M})\right)}_{(a)}+\underbrace{d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{G}^{M})\right)}_{(b)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{G}^{M}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)}_{(c)}.

By
17
, we know that there is
\textsf{C}>0
such that
\limsup~(b)\leq\textsf{C}\beta(\tilde{m})^{1/15}
as
n\to\infty
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (\star)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (a)+\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (c)+\textsf{C}\beta(\tilde{m})^{1/15}.

We may now apply
29
with
\tilde{m}
to say that for some
\textsf{C}_{d}>0
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (\star)\leq 2\textsf{C}_{d}\frac{m}{M}\sqrt{M+m}+\textsf{C}\beta(\tilde{m})^{1/15}=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{-1/2}+\textsf{C}\beta(\tilde{m})^{1/15}\right).

(34)

As this holds for every
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}+
, we take
M\to\infty
to obtain that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ (\star)\leq\textsf{C}\beta(\tilde{m})^{1/15}.

(35)

Finally noting that this holds for an arbitrary
\tilde{m}
gives us the desired result.

\jmlrQED

Appendix G
Proof of
Theorem

2
(
6
) and
Theorem

3
(
8
): Test risk universality

In this section, we prove the second equation of both
concerning test risk universality, as both share the same proof once training risk universality is proved. We focus on presenting the proof for the 0-1 loss, i.e. the test risk
R_{\rm test}
is defined with

\displaystyle\ell_{\rm test}(X_{\rm new}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\,,\,X_{\rm new}^{\intercal}\beta^{*})\;\coloneqq\;\mathbb{I}\Big\{\;\mathbb{I}\Big\{\sigma(X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta})\geq\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{2}$}}\Big\}=\mathbb{I}\big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\big\}\;\Big\}

for both
\hat{\beta}=\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X})
and
\hat{\beta}=\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{G})
. As our proof strategy relies on approximating
\ell_{\rm test}
by the
1
-Lipschitz functions in
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
, the same proof also works if
\ell_{\rm test}
is already Lipschitz. Therefore the result also applies to any locally Lipschitz
\ell_{\rm test}
, which is Lipschitz over the compact set
\mathcal{S}_{p}
.

Proof of
2
(
6
) and
Theorem

3
(
8
)

Recall that
\sigma(x)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}
. Our test loss can then be re-expressed as

\displaystyle R_{\text{test}}(\hat{\beta})\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{\mathbb{I}\big\{\sigma(X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta})\geq\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{2}$}}\big\}=\mathbb{I}\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\}\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{\mathbb{I}\big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\big\}=\mathbb{I}\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\}\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,+\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}<0\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}<0\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]\;,

(36)

and by a similar argument,

\displaystyle R_{{\text{test}}}^{G}(\hat{\beta})\;=\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,+\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}<0\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}<0\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]\;.

For convenience, we denote the random
\mathbb{R}^{2}
vectors

\displaystyle V_{X}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;(X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}})^{\intercal}

and

\displaystyle V_{G}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;(G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}})^{\intercal}\;.

We first perform a standard smoothing of the indicator function. By Lemma 34 of
Huang et al. (2023)
, for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
, there exists a continuously differentiable function
h_{\tau;\delta}
such that
h_{\tau+\delta;\delta}(x)\leq\mathbb{I}\{x\geq\tau\}\leq h_{\tau;\delta}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and that
\partial h_{\tau;\delta}
is bounded in norm by
\delta^{-1}
. Moreover
h_{\tau;\delta}
takes value in
[0,1]
. We use this to construct the
\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
function
\tilde{h}_{\tau;\delta}(x,y)\coloneqq h_{\tau;\delta}(x)\,h_{\tau;\delta}(y)
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(x,y)\;\leq\;\mathbb{I}\{x\geq 0,y\geq 0\}\;=\;\mathbb{I}\{x\geq 0\}\,\mathbb{I}\{y\geq 0\}\;\leq\;\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(x,y)\;.

This implies that for every
\delta>0
, almost surely

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,-\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{X})-\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(V_{G})\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{X})-\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{G})+\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{G})-\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(V_{G})\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{X})-\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{G})+\mathbb{I}\{(V_{G})_{1}\geq-\delta\,,\,(V_{G})_{2}\geq-\delta\}-\mathbb{I}\{(V_{G})_{1}\geq\delta\,,\,(V_{G})_{2}\geq\delta\}\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{X})-\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{G})\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{1}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{2}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)\;.

In the last inequality, we have noted that if
(V_{G})_{1},(V_{G})_{2}\geq-\delta
is true and yet
(V_{G})_{1},(V_{G})_{2}\geq\delta
is false, we must have either
(V_{G})_{1}\in[-\delta,\delta)
or
(V_{G})_{2}\in[-\delta,\delta)
. Now let
\delta\in(0,1]
. Notice that
\delta\,\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
, where
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
is defined in
Assumption

6
. Also note that
\hat{\beta}
is independent of
X_{\rm new}
and
G_{\rm new}
in
V_{X}
and
V_{G}
. This implies according to
Assumption

6
that

\displaystyle\,\big|\,\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{X})\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{G})\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]\,\big|\,\;

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\delta}$}}\,\sup\nolimits_{f\in\mathcal{\tilde{F}}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[f(X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta},X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}})\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[f(G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta},G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}})\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\delta}$}}\,\sup\nolimits_{f\in\mathcal{\tilde{F}}}\,\sup\nolimits_{\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[f(X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta},X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}})\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[f(G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta},G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}})\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\;\overset{(a)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\delta}$}}\,\sup\nolimits_{f\in\mathcal{\tilde{F}}}\,\sup\nolimits_{\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[f(X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta},X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*})\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[f(G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta},G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*})\Big]\Big|\;\eqqcolon\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\delta}$}}\Delta_{n}

In
(a)
, we have used a conditioning on
\varepsilon_{\text{new}}
, moved the suprema and the norm inside the expectation over
\varepsilon_{\text{new}}
and observed that the function
f({\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}})\in\mathcal{\tilde{F}}
almost surely. Substituting this into the above yields that, almost surely

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,-\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\delta}$}}\Delta_{n}+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{1}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{2}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)\;.

By a similar argument, we can obtain that almost surely

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,-\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{G})-\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(V_{X})\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(V_{G})-\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(V_{X})+\tilde{h}_{0;\delta}(V_{G})-\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(V_{G})\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(V_{X})-\tilde{h}_{\delta;\delta}(V_{G})\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big]+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{1}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{2}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\frac{1}{\delta}\Delta_{n}+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{1}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{2}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)\;.

Combining the two bounds implies that, almost surely,

\displaystyle(\star)\;\coloneqq\;

\displaystyle\;\Big|\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,-\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]\,\Big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2}{\delta}$}}\Delta_{n}+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{1}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{2}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)\;.

To control the probability terms, notice that conditioning on
\hat{\beta}
,
(V_{G})_{1}=G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\,|\,\hat{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\hat{\beta}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\hat{\beta})
and
(V_{G})_{2}\,|\,\varepsilon_{\text{new}},\hat{\beta}\,\sim\mathcal{N}(-\varepsilon_{\text{new}},{\beta^{*}}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\beta^{*})
. Therefore by a standard anti-concentration result for Gaussians (see e.g.
Carbery and Wright (2001)
), there is an absolute constant
C^{\prime\prime}>0
such that, almost surely,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{1}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big((V_{G})_{2}\in[-\delta,\delta)\,\big|\,\hat{\beta}\,,\,\varepsilon_{\rm new}\big)\;\leq\;C^{\prime\prime}\delta\,\bigg(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\hat\beta^\intercal\Sigma_{\rm new }\hat\beta}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{{\beta^*}^\intercal\Sigma_{\rm new} \beta^* }$}}\bigg)\;.

Meanwhile by
Assumption

7
, for every
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(D_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{G})>0)\;\rightarrow\;1\;,\qquad\text{ where }\quad D_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{G})\;\coloneqq\;\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{p}\,,\,|(\beta^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\text{new}}\beta)^{1/2}\,-\,\bar{\chi}|>\epsilon}\hskip-7.0pt\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})-\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\;,

and by the universality of the training risk (
Theorem

2
(
5
) or
Theorem

3
(
7
)), we also have
\mathbb{P}(D_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{X})>0)\rightarrow 1
. This implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\,\big|(\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X})^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\text{new}}\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X}))^{1/2}-\bar{\chi}\big|\leq\epsilon\,\big)\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}(D_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{X})>0)\;\rightarrow\;1\;,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\,\big|(\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{G})^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\text{new}}\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{G}))^{1/2}-\bar{\chi}\big|\leq\epsilon\,\big)\;=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}(D_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{G})>0)\;\rightarrow\;1\;.

(37)

In other words, for both
\hat{\beta}=\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X})
and
\hat{\beta}=\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{G})
,
\hat{\beta}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\hat{\beta}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\bar{\chi}^{2}
in probability. Moreover,
\bar{\chi}>0
and
{\beta^{*}}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\beta^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\chi_{*}^{2}>0
by
Assumption

7
. This allows us to consider a rare event

\displaystyle E_{\chi}\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\Big\{\hat{\beta}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\hat{\beta}<\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\bar\chi^2}{2}$}}\,,\,{\beta^{*}}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\beta^{*}<\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\chi_*^2}{2}$}}\Big\}\;\qquad\;\text{ such that }\quad\mathbb{P}(E_{\chi})\;\rightarrow\;0\;.

Denoting
E_{\chi}^{c}
as the complement of
E_{\chi}
, we obtain that for any
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|(\star)|>\epsilon^{\prime})\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}\bigg(\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2}{\delta}$}}\Delta_{n}+C^{\prime\prime}\delta\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\hat\beta^\intercal\Sigma_{\rm new }\hat\beta}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{{\beta^*}^\intercal\Sigma_{\rm new} \beta^* }$}}\Big)\,>\,\epsilon^{\prime}\,\bigg)

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}\bigg(\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2}{\delta}$}}\Delta_{n}+C^{\prime\prime}\delta\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{\hat\beta^\intercal\Sigma_{\rm new }\hat\beta}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{{\beta^*}^\intercal\Sigma_{\rm new} \beta^* }$}}\Big)\,>\,\epsilon^{\prime}\,\Big|\,E_{\chi}^{c}\bigg)+\mathbb{P}(E_{\chi})

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{I}\Big\{\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2}{\delta}$}}\Delta_{n}+C^{\prime\prime}\delta\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2}{\bar\chi^2}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2}{\chi_*^2}$}}\Big)\,>\,\epsilon^{\prime}\Big\}+\mathbb{P}(E_{\chi})\;.

By
Assumption

6
,
\Delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
. Since the above is valid for any
\delta
, whose choice is independent of
\epsilon^{\prime}
, we can choose
\delta=\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}
, which implies that the above converge to zero. In other words, we have shown that

\displaystyle\Big|\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,-\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\geq 0\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}\geq 0\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]\,\Big|\;\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\;0

for both
\hat{\beta}=\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X})
and
\hat{\beta}=\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{G})
. By an exactly analogous argument, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}<0\,,\,X_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}<0\Big\}\,\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\mathbb{I}\Big\{G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}<0\,,\,G_{\text{new}}^{\intercal}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{\text{new}}<0\Big\}\,\Big|\,\hat{\beta}\Big]\,\Big|\;\rightarrow\;0\;.

In view of (
36
), we can use a triangle inequality to obtain that

\displaystyle|R_{\text{test}}(\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X}))-R_{{\text{test}}}^{G}(\beta(\mathbf{X}))|\;\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle\;0

and

\displaystyle|R_{\text{test}}(\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{G}))-R_{{\text{test}}}^{G}(\beta(\mathbf{G}))|\;\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle\;0\;.

Meanwhile, note that
R_{{\text{test}}}^{G}(\hat{\beta})
depends on
\hat{\beta}
only through the mean-zero conditionally Gaussian variable
G_{\rm new}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}
, which is completely characterized by
\text{\rm Var}[G_{\rm new}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\,|\,\hat{\beta}]=\hat{\beta}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\hat{\beta}
. In view of (
37
), both
\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X})^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X})
and
\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{G})^{\intercal}\Sigma_{\rm new}\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{G})
converge in probaility to the same constant
\bar{\chi}^{2}
. This implies

\displaystyle|R_{\text{test}}^{G}(\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X}))-R_{{\text{test}}}^{G}(\beta(\mathbf{G}))|\;\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle\;0\;,

which in particular implies the desired statement that
|R_{\text{test}}(\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{X}))-R_{{\text{test}}}^{G}(\beta(\mathbf{G}))|\,\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\,0

\jmlrQED

Appendix H
Important Lemmas

In this section, we present the statements and proofs of the various lemmas used to prove our main theorems.

H.1
Auxiliary Lemmas

The lemma and its corollary aim to bound the expectation of the maximum possible norm of our signal
\mathbf{X}\beta
, conditional on the fact that a Bernstein-like Inequality holds.

Lemma 19

(Operator Norm Bound)
.

Let
(\mathbf{Y}_{i})
be a sequence of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
-valued random vectors, and let
\textnormal{{R}}>0
. Suppose that
\frac{n}{p}\to\kappa
and that there exist constants
\textnormal{{K}},\textnormal{{C}}_{1},\textnormal{{c}}_{2},\textnormal{{C}}_{3}
such that

1.

\sup_{i\leq n}\|\textnormal{Var}(\mathbf{Y}_{i})\|_{\textnormal{op}}\leq\tfrac{1}{p}\textnormal{{K}}\
.

2.

For all
\beta\in\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{B}_{p}(0,\textnormal{{R}}\sqrt{p})
and
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{n}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta)^{2}-\mathbb{E}((\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta)^{2})\Big|\geq t\right)\leq\textnormal{{C}}_{1}\exp\left(-\textnormal{{c}}_{2}n\left(\frac{t}{\textnormal{{C}}_{3}\textnormal{{R}}^{2}}\wedge\frac{t^{2}}{\textnormal{{C}}_{3}^{2}\textnormal{{R}}^{4}}\right)\right).

(38)

Then there exists
\textnormal{{C}}_{\textnormal{{R}}}>0
depending on
R
and the constants above such that for
n
sufficiently large,

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}\right]\leq\textnormal{{C}}_{\textnormal{{R}}}p,

where
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}
is as defined in (
12
).

Proof
Let
\beta\in\mathcal{S}
, meaning by definition
\|\beta\|_{2}\leq\textsf{R}\sqrt{p}
. Note that by the first statement of the lemma, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{Y}\beta\|^{2}\right]=n\beta^{\intercal}\overline{\Sigma}_{n}\beta\leq n\textsf{R}^{2}\textsf{K},

where
\overline{\Sigma}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\text{Var}(\mathbf{Y}_{i})
. Now, we can note that for
s>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\beta\|^{2}\geq s\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}\geq\frac{s-n\beta^{\intercal}\overline{\Sigma}_{n}\beta}{n}\right),

where
W_{i}:=(\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta)^{2}-\beta^{\intercal}\text{Var}(\mathbf{Y}_{i})\beta
. If we let
\textsf{M}_{\textsf{R}}:=\textsf{R}^{2}(\textsf{K}+\textsf{C}_{3})
, then by (
38
), we obtain that for
s>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\beta\|^{2}\geq n\textsf{M}_{\textsf{R}}(s+1)\right)\leq\textsf{C}_{1}\cdot\exp\left[-{\textsf{c}_{2}n}\left(s\wedge{s^{2}}\right)\right],

(39)

which follows from noting that

\displaystyle\frac{n\textsf{M}_{\textsf{R}}(s+1)-n\beta^{\intercal}\overline{\Sigma}_{n}\beta}{n\textsf{C}_{3}\textsf{R}^{2}}\geq\frac{\textsf{M}_{\textsf{R}}(s+1)-\textsf{M}_{\textsf{R}}}{\textsf{M}_{\textsf{R}}}=s.

Now let
\varepsilon>0
, and define
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
to be a minimal
\varepsilon\sqrt{p}
-net of
\mathcal{S}
. Also, for
t>0
, define the quantity

\eta_{t}:=\frac{\textsf{C}_{3}\sqrt{p}+t}{\sqrt{n}}\quad\text{for}\quad\textsf{C}_{3}:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\textsf{c}_{2}}\log\left(1+\frac{3\textsf{R}}{\varepsilon}\right)}.

Then if we set
s=\eta_{t}\vee\eta^{2}_{t}
in (
39
), by a union bound we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\|\mathbf{Y}\beta\|^{2}\geq n\textsf{M}_{\textsf{R}}\left((\eta_{t}\vee\eta_{t}^{2})+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\textsf{C}_{1}\absolutevalue{\mathcal S_{\varepsilon}}\exp\left[-\textsf{c}_{2}n\cdot\eta_{t}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\textsf{C}_{1}\left(\frac{3\textsf{R}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{p}\exp\left[-\textsf{c}_{2}(\textsf{C}_{3}^{2}p+t^{2})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\textsf{C}_{1}e^{-\textsf{c}_{2}t^{2}},

where
(i)
is via the fact that for any
x\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle(x\vee x^{2})\wedge(x\vee x^{2})^{2}=x^{2},

(ii)
is via Corollary 4.2.13 of
Vershynin (2018)
bounding the cardinality of a minimal
\varepsilon
-net, and
(iii)
is from the definition of
\eta_{t}
and
\textsf{C}_{3}
. Now we may bound the error between the supremum on the whole space and the supremum on the
\varepsilon
-net by applying a similar technique to Lemma 4.4.1 of
Vershynin (2018)
, which gives

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{S}}\|\mathbf{Y}\beta\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{1-2\varepsilon}\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}}\|\mathbf{Y}\beta\|^{2}.

We conclude that, for
\textsf{A}:=\sqrt{\textsf{M}_{\textsf{R}}/(1-2\varepsilon)}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}\geq n\textsf{A}^{2}\left((\eta_{t}\vee\eta_{t}^{2})+1\right)\right)\leq\textsf{C}_{1}e^{-\textsf{c}_{2}t^{2}}.

(40)

Now, let us define the two events

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\left\{\frac{\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}}{n\textsf{A}^{2}}-1\leq\eta_{t}\vee\eta_{t}^{2}\right\},\quad\quad\mathcal{E}_{2}:=\left\{\frac{\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}}{\sqrt{n}\textsf{A}}\leq 1\right\},

where we note that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is exactly the high-probability event of (
40
). Then we can first see that, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\implies\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\leq\textsf{A}\sqrt{n},

(41)

which is simply from the definition of the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. On the other hand, for the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}}{\sqrt{n}\textsf{A}}-1\right)^{2}\vee\ \absolutevalue{\frac{\|\bY\|_{\mathcal S}}{\sqrt{n}\textsf{A}} - 1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\absolutevalue{\frac{\|\bY\|_{\mathcal S}^2}{n\textsf{A}^2} - 1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}\frac{\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}}{n\textsf{A}^{2}}-1

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\eta_{t}\vee\eta_{t}^{2},

where
(i)
is from the fact that
(x-y)^{2}\vee|x-y|\leq|x^{2}-y^{2}|
for
x,y>0
and
x+y\geq 1
,
(ii)
follows from
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
, and
(iii)
from
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Since we also know that

\displaystyle(x\vee x^{2})\leq(y\vee y^{2})\implies x\leq y\quad\text{ for }\quad x,y\geq 0,

we can say that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}\implies\absolutevalue{\frac{\|\bY\|_{\mathcal S}}{\sqrt{n}\textsf{A}} - 1}\leq\eta_{t}

\displaystyle\implies\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\leq\textsf{A}\left(\sqrt{n}+\textsf{C}_{3}\sqrt{p}+t\right).

(42)

Combining (
41
) and (
42
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}=(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2})\cup(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c})

\displaystyle\implies\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}\leq\tilde{\textsf{A}}\left(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p}+t\right),

where
\tilde{\textsf{A}}=\textsf{A}(\textsf{C}_{3}+1)
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\geq\tilde{\textsf{A}}\left(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p}+t\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{C}_{1}e^{-\textsf{c}_{2}t^{2}}.

If we set the variable
y=\tilde{\textsf{A}}^{2}\left(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p}+t\right)^{2}
, then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\geq\sqrt{y}\right)\leq\textsf{C}_{1}\cdot\exp\left[-\textsf{c}_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{y}}{\tilde{\textsf{A}}}-\sqrt{n}-\sqrt{p}\right)^{2}\right]

whenever
\sqrt{y}\geq\tilde{\textsf{A}}\left(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p}\right)
.
This lets us conclude via the tail-integral formula that, after setting
\varepsilon=1/4
for example,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}\geq y\right)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\geq\sqrt{y}\right)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tilde{\textsf{A}}^{2}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p})^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\geq\sqrt{y}\right)\,\text{d}y+\int_{\tilde{\textsf{A}}^{2}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p})^{2}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\geq\sqrt{y}\right)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\tilde{\textsf{A}}^{2}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p})^{2}}1\,\text{d}y+\textsf{C}_{1}\int_{\tilde{\textsf{A}}^{2}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p})^{2}}^{\infty}\exp\left[-\textsf{c}_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{y}}{\tilde{\textsf{A}}}-\sqrt{n}-\sqrt{p}\right)^{2}\right]\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\textsf{A}}^{2}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p})^{2}+\tilde{\textsf{A}}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\textsf{c}_{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p})}{\sqrt{\textsf{c}_{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{C}_{\textsf{R}}\cdot p,

for

\displaystyle\textsf{C}_{\textsf{R}}\propto\textsf{R}^{2}\log(1 + 12\textsf R).

\jmlrQED

Now that we have given a bound on this scaled operator norm, we can use this to obtain a bound in the specific cases of block dependence,
m
-dependence, and mixing.

Corollary 20

(Dependent Norm Bound)
.

Let
(\mathbf{X}_{i})
be a sequence of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
-valued random vectors satisfying Assumptions
2
-
4
. Then setting
\mathcal{S}
in
19
as either
\mathcal{B}_{p}(0,\sqrt{p})
or
\mathcal{S}_{p}
as in (
4
), the following holds for
n
sufficiently large:

(1)

If
(\mathbf{X}_{i})
satisfies Assumption
1
(block-dependence), then
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}]\leq\textnormal{{C}}kp
.

(2)

If
(\mathbf{X}_{i})
satisfies Assumption
8
(i) (
m
-dependence), then
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}]\leq\textnormal{{C}}mp
.

(3)

If
(\mathbf{X}_{i})
satisfies Assumption
8
(ii) (
\beta
-mixing) and there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\textnormal{{S}}:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\beta(\ell)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2+\varepsilon}}<\infty,

then
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}]\leq\textnormal{{C}}\textnormal{{S}}p
. Furthermore, for
(\mathbf{G}_{i})
also satisfying Assumptions
2
-
4
with
\mathbf{G}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\textnormal{Var}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\right)
and
\textnormal{cov}(\mathbf{G})=\textnormal{cov}(\mathbf{X})
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{G}\|_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}]\leq\textnormal{{C}}\textnormal{{S}}p.

Proof
We display the proofs for
\mathcal{S}_{p}
, as those for the ball of radius
\sqrt{p}
are identical.

(1)
: Note that
(1)
follows from
(2)
, as block-dependence with block parameter
k
is a special case of
m
-dependence with
m=k
.

(2)
: We check that the two conditions of
19
hold. For the first one, note that by the sub-Gaussian Assumption
3
and
44
, we have

\displaystyle\|\text{Var}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\|_{\text{op}}\leq\frac{\textsf{C}_{1}\textsf{K}_{X}^{2}}{n}\leq\frac{\textsf{B}}{p}

for some
\textsf{C}_{1},\textsf{B}>0
and
n
sufficiently large. For the second condition, by sub-Gaussianity and the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{p}
, we have that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta)^{2}\|_{\psi_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\|\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{\textsf{K}_{X}^{2}\|\beta\|^{2}}{n}\leq\textsf{C}_{2}\textsf{L}^{2}

(43)

for
n
sufficiently large, where
(i)
is by Lemma 2.7.6 of
Vershynin (2018)
. Thus we may apply
36
with
Z_{i}:=(X_{i}^{\intercal}\beta^{2})-\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{i}^{\intercal}\beta^{2})\right]
and
\textsf{K}=\textsf{C}_{2}\textsf{L}^{2}
. This gives us our second condition, namely (
38
), with the constants
(\textsf{R},\textsf{K},\textsf{C}_{1},\textsf{c}_{2},\textsf{C}_{3})
set as
(\textsf{L},\textsf{B},4,cm^{-1},\textsf{C}_{2})
. For the Gaussin case, the first condition holds for the exact same reason. For the second condition, we again use
39
, which allows us to satisfy (
38
) with the constants
(\textsf{R},\textsf{K},\textsf{C}_{1},\textsf{c}_{2},\textsf{C}_{3})
set as
(\textsf{L},\textsf{B},2,c\textsf{S}^{-1},\textsf{K}_{X}\underline{c}^{-1})
, respectively.

(4)
: As above, the first condition of
19
holds for the same reason as
(2)
and
(3)
. The second condition holds by the second statement of
39
, which allows us to satisfy (
38
) with the constants
(\textsf{R},\textsf{K},\textsf{C}_{1},\textsf{c}_{2},\textsf{C}_{3})
set as
(\textsf{L},\textsf{B},2,c\textsf{S}^{-1},c_{2}\left(\tilde{C}_{2}\wedge\tilde{C}_{2}^{\prime}\right))
.

\jmlrQED

Next, recall that we have smoothed our labels
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
by taking a convolution of the sign function with a mollifier
\zeta_{\gamma}
as in (
14
). This next lemma shows that the derivative of this convolution grows at a rate inversely proportional to the smoothing factor,
\gamma
.

Lemma 21

(Smoothed Label Derivative Bound)
.

Define
\eta_{i}^{\prime}:={\bm{1}_{\gamma}^{\pm}}^{\prime}(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i})
. Then

\absolutevalue{\eta_i'}\leq 3\gamma^{-1}.

Proof
Recall by the properties of convolution that a derivative can be “absorbed” into the convolution like so:

\displaystyle\eta_{i}^{\prime}:={\bm{1}^{\pm}_{\gamma}}^{\prime}(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i})

\displaystyle=(\bm{1}^{\pm}*\zeta_{\gamma})^{\prime}(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i})

\displaystyle=(\bm{1}^{\pm}*\zeta_{\gamma}^{\prime})(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\gamma}^{\gamma}\bm{1}^{\pm}(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i}-t)\zeta_{\gamma}^{\prime}(t)\,\text{d}t.

We may thus bound

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\eta_i'}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\int_{-\gamma}^\gamma\bm 1^\pm(a_{\mathcal B_i}^\intercal X_{\mathcal B_i}\beta^* - \varepsilon_i-t)\zeta_\gamma'(t)\,\text{d}t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\gamma}^{\gamma}\absolutevalue{\zeta_\gamma'(t)}\,\text{d}t

\displaystyle=2\textsf{C}\gamma^{2}\int_{-\gamma}^{\gamma}\frac{|t|}{(t^{2}-\gamma^{2})^{2}}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{t^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\right)\,\text{d}t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}4\textsf{C}\gamma^{2}\int_{0}^{\gamma}\frac{t}{(t^{2}-\gamma^{2})^{2}}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{t^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\right)\,\text{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{e}\textsf{C},

where
(i)
is from the fact that the integrand is even. Thus it suffices to bound
C
, which is the integrating constant of our mollifier
\zeta_{\gamma}
. We may lower bound the integral like so:

\displaystyle\textsf{C}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\gamma}^{\gamma}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{t^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\right)\,\text{d}t

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\gamma}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{t^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\right)\,\text{d}t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\geq}}2\int_{0}^{\gamma/2}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{t^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\right)\,\text{d}t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\geq}}2\int_{0}^{\gamma/2}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{(\gamma/2)^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\right)\,\text{d}t

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot e^{-4/3}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\gamma}{4},

where
(i)
follows from the fact that the integrand is non-negative, and
(ii)
from the fact that it is a decreasing function, as it has derivative

\displaystyle\frac{-2t\gamma^{2}}{(t^{2}-\gamma^{2})^{2}}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{t^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\right)<0\quad\text{ for }\quad 0<t<\gamma.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\eta_i'}\leq\frac{2}{e}\cdot\frac{4}{\gamma}\leq\frac{3}{\gamma}.

\jmlrQED

H.2
General lemmas

H.2.1
Replacing the True Minimum with a Smoothed & Discretized Minimum

To prove that the two minimum risks are close in distribution to one another, we must first smooth the labels, and then also discretize the parameter space that we are taking the minimum over. The following two lemmas show that the error incurred by these two approximations is negligible in the limit.

Lemma 22

(Smoothing the Risk)
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{G}
satisfy Assumptions
2
–
4
. Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Then there exists
\textnormal{{C}}>0
such that for
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\textnormal{{C}}\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\gamma}

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G}),\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\textnormal{{C}}\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\gamma}.

Proof
We show the proof only for
\mathbf{X}
, and note that the exact same technique holds for
\mathbf{G}
. Since
h\in\mathcal{H}
is Lipschitz, we know that if
\tilde{\beta}
and
\tilde{\beta}_{\gamma}
are the minimizers of
\hat{R}_{n}
and
\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}
on
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
respectively, then

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X})\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbb{E}\left[h\left(\hat{R}_{n}(\tilde{\beta};\mathbf{X})\right)-h\left(\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\tilde{\beta}_{\gamma};\mathbf{X})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\|h^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\tilde\beta; \bX) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta_\gamma; \bX)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\tilde\beta; \bX) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta_\gamma; \bX)},

where the last line is by definition of
\mathcal{H}
. To control this term, we can note that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\tilde\beta; \bX) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta_\gamma; \bX)}\leq\max\Big\{

\displaystyle\underbrace{\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\tilde\beta; \bX) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX)}}_{(a)},\ \ \underbrace{\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\tilde\beta_\gamma; \bX) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta_\gamma; \bX)}}_{(b)}\Big\}.

We bound the first term
(a)
like so:

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\tilde\beta; \bX) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX)}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\absolutevalue{\log\l(1 + e^{-y_iX_i^\intercal\tilde\beta}\r) - \log\l(1 + e^{-\eta_iX_i^\intercal\tilde\beta}\r)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\absolutevalue{X_i^\intercal\tilde\beta}|y_{i}-\eta_{i}|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbf{X}\tilde{\beta}\|\ \|\bm{y}-\bm{\eta}\|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}\|\bm{y}-\bm{\eta}\|,

where
(i)
uses that
0\leq\omega_{i}\leq 1
for all
i\leq n
,
(ii)
comes from treating the loss as a function of the label and Taylor expanding,
(iii)
is from Cauchy-Schwarz, and
(iv)
is from the definition of
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}
. Applying Cauchy-Schwarz once more we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(a)]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}\mathbb{E}\|\bm{y}-\bm{\eta}\|^{2}}.

From here we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\bm{y}-\bm{\eta}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[(y_{i}-\eta_{i})^{2}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[(y_{i}-\eta_{i})^{2}\bm{1}_{|a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i}|\leq\gamma}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(|a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i}|\leq\gamma\mid X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}n\gamma,

where the indicator in
(i)
is introduced as

\displaystyle y_{i}-\eta_{i}\neq 0\iff|a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i}|\leq\gamma,

(ii)
is because
(y_{i}-\eta_{i})^{2}\leq 1
, and
(iii)
from

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\varepsilon_{i}|\leq\gamma\mid X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}+\gamma)-\sigma(a_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}^{\intercal}X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\beta^{*}-\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\|\sigma^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(a)]\leq\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}}}{n}\sqrt{n\gamma}\leq\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}^{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\gamma}

for
n
sufficiently large. To finish, note that this exact string of inequalities also holds for
\mathbb{E}[(b)]
.

\jmlrQED

Now that we have bounded the difference between the original risk and smoothed risk in terms of the smoothing parameter
\gamma
, we can show that universality for the smoothed risk reduces to universality for the smooth minimum
f_{\delta}
.

Lemma 23

(Discretization & Smooth-Min)
.

Let
\alpha,\delta>0
. Suppose that
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{G}
satisfy Assumptions
2
-
4
. Write
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{B}(0,\sqrt{p})
. Then there exists
\textnormal{{C}}>0
such that for
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{X}),f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{G})\right)+\textsf{C}\Big(\delta+\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\|\textbf{X}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}}\right]\vee\mathbb{E}\left[\|\textbf{G}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}}\right]}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\Big)

Proof
By the Triangle Inequality, we know that

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\delta}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X})\right)

\displaystyle+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\delta}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{X})\right)

\displaystyle+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{X}),f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(f_{\delta}(\alpha,\mathbf{G}),\min_{\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\delta}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle+d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\delta}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G}),\min_{\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{G})\right)

\displaystyle=:\mathbb{D}_{1}+\mathbb{D}_{2}+\mathbb{D}_{3}+\mathbb{D}_{4}+\mathbb{D}_{5}.

For
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, let
\tilde{\beta}
be the minimizer of the risk on
\mathcal{\tilde{S}}
,
\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}
the closest point to it on the
\delta\sqrt{p}
-net
\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}
, and
\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}
the minimizer of the risk on
\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[h\l(\hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX)\r) - h\l(\hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta'; \bX)\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{h\l(\hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX)\r) - h\l(\hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta'; \bX)\r)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta'; \bX)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta_\delta; \bX)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\nu}\absolutevalue{\lip\nabla\hat R_n^\gamma(\nu; \bX), \tilde\beta- \tilde\beta_\delta\rip}\right],

where
(i)
is from the definition of
\mathcal{H}
and
(ii)
is because

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\tilde{\beta}_{\delta})\geq\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\tilde{\beta}^{\prime})

by definition. The gradient of our risk has coordinates

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu_{j}}\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\nu;\mathbf{X})=\frac{\lambda}{n}\nu_{j}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\eta_{i}X_{ij}\sigma_{i\nu},

and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\nu}\absolutevalue{\lip\nu, \tilde\beta- \tilde\beta_\delta\rip}\right]+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\nu}\absolutevalue{\l\lip\sumi{n}\eta_i\omega_iX_{i}\sigma_{i\nu}, \tilde\beta- \tilde\beta_\delta\r\rip}\mathbb{I}(\mathbf{X}\in E_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\lambda\textsf{L}\delta p}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\nu}\absolutevalue{\sumi{n}\eta_i\omega_i\sigma_{i\nu}\l\lip X_i, \tilde\beta- \tilde\beta_\delta\r\rip}\mathbb{I}(\mathbf{X}\in E_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\frac{\lambda\textsf{L}\delta p}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\nu}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}^{2}\sigma_{i\nu}^{2}\omega_{i}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle X_{i},\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}\right\rangle^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\lambda\textsf{L}\delta p}{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle X_{i},\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}\right\rangle^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}\frac{\lambda\textsf{L}\delta p}{n}+\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\textbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\right]

Above,
(i)
is from the fact that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\lip\nu, \tilde\beta- \tilde\beta_\delta\rip}\leq\|\nu\|\ \|\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}\|\leq\textsf{L}\sqrt{p}\delta\sqrt{p}=\textsf{L}\delta p,

(ii)
is a result of Cauchy-Schwarz,
(iii)
uses that
\eta_{i},\omega_{i},\sigma_{i,v}\leq 1
for all
i\leq n
,
(iv)
is via the definition of
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}}
and the fact that
\delta^{-1}(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{\beta}_{\delta})\in\mathcal{S}
. To bound
\mathbb{D}_{2}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{f_{\delta}(\alpha, \bX) -\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde S}_{ \delta}}\hat R_n^\gamma(\beta; \bX)}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\frac{-1}{n\alpha}\log\l(\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde S}_{\delta}}\exp\l[-n\alpha\hat R_n^\gamma(\beta; \bX)\r]\r) - \hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\alpha}\absolutevalue{\log\l(\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde S}_{\delta}}\exp\l[-n\alpha\hat R_n^\gamma(\beta; \bX)\r]\r) - \log\l(\exp\l[-n\alpha\hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX)\r]\r)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\alpha}\absolutevalue{\log\l(\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde S}_{\delta}}\exp\l[-n\alpha\l(\hat R_n^\gamma(\beta; \bX)-\hat R_n^\gamma(\tilde\beta; \bX)\r)\r]\r)}

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{n\alpha}\log\absolutevalue{\mathcal{\tilde S}_{\delta}},

(44)

where the last line follows from the fact that
\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta;\mathbf{X})-\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\tilde{\beta};\mathbf{X})
is always non-negative for
\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}
by definition, and is zero at least once, meaning when we multiply by
-n\alpha
they will always be either zero or strictly negative. This means the sum inside of the logarithm lies in
\left(1,\absolutevalue{\mathcal{\tilde S}_{\delta}}\right)
. By Proposition 4.2.12 of
Vershynin (2018)
, we can say that since
\mathcal{S}_{p}\subseteq B_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}(\bm{0},\textsf{L}\sqrt{p})
, then

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\mathcal{\tilde S}_{\delta}}\leq\left(\frac{3\textsf{L}\sqrt{p}}{\delta\sqrt{p}}\right)^{p}=\left(\frac{3\textsf{L}}{\delta}\right)^{p},

and so combining this with (
44
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{2}\leq\frac{p}{n\alpha}\log\left(\frac{3\textsf{L}}{\delta}\right)\leq\textsf{C}_{3}\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)

for
n
sufficiently large. We finish by noting that
\mathbb{D}_{4}
and
\mathbb{D}_{5}
have the exact same bounds as
\mathbb{D}_{2}
and
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, respectively.

\jmlrQED

H.3
Main lemmas under independence under partial block independence.

Let
i\leq n
be an index. Throughout this subsection we will assume that the block
(\mathbf{X}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j}))_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
is independent from
(\mathbf{X}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j}))_{j\not\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
where
\mathcal{B}_{i}
is the block of size
k
that contains
i
.

H.3.1
Main Lemma

In the next result, we recall the notation that
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}\equiv\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{i}(t)=\cos(t)\mathbf{X}_{i}-\sin(t)\mathbf{G}_{i}
for
t\in[0,1]
, where we abbreviate the dependence on
t
for the simplicity of presentation in the proof.

Lemma 24
.

Let
i\leq n
. Let
(\mathbf{X}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j}))
and
(\mathbf{G}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{G}_{j}))
be generated under Assumptions
2
-
5
and that
\rm{Var}(\mathbf{G})=\rm{Var}(\mathbf{X})
. In addition, suppose that the block
(\mathbf{X}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j}))_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
is independent from
(\mathbf{X}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j}))_{j\not\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
. For every
t\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\absolutevalue{\E\l[ -h'\l(f_\delta(\bU)\r)\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)\rip\r]}\leq\tau

(45)

and

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\absolutevalue{\E\l[ -h'\l(f_\delta(\bU)\r)\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i,L}(\beta)\rip\r]}\leq\tau

(46)

where we defined
\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta):=\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\mathbb{I}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|,|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|\leq L)

Proof
We note that it is enough to show the desired result for
\mathcal{D}_{i,L}
as
Eq.

45
directly follows by taking
L=\infty
.

Firstly by adding and subtracting the quantity
h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}^{ik}))
we obtain

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\E\l[-h'\l(f_\delta(\bU)\r)\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i,L}(\beta)\rip\r]}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\l(h'(f_\delta(\bU)) - h'(f_\delta(\bU^{ik}))\r)\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i,L}(\beta)\rip}}_{(a)}+\underbrace{\absolutevalue{\E\l[h'(f_\delta(\bU^{ik}))\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i,L}(\beta)\rip\r]}}_{(b)}.

For the term
(a)
, we use Cauchy-Schwarz to say that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\l(h'(f_\delta(\bU)) - h'(f_\delta(\bU^{ik}))\r)\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i,L}(\beta)\rip}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}))-h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}^{ik}))\right]^{2}}}_{(a_{1})}\cdot\underbrace{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)\rangle^{2}}}_{(a_{2})}.

To control these two terms, we first apply
25
to
(a_{1})
to obtain

\displaystyle(a_{1})\leq\sqrt{\frac{\textsf{C}_{1}k^{2}}{n}}=\frac{\textsf{C}_{2}k}{\sqrt{n}},

(47)

and then apply
26
to
(a_{2})
to say that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right\rangle_{i,k}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left(\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)^{2}\right\rangle_{i,k}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)^{2}\right]\right\rangle_{i,k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)^{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{\leq}}\textnormal{{C}}_{1}(k,\alpha,\gamma),

(48)

where
(i)
comes from noting that, for a general function
g
,

\displaystyle\langle\textsf{g}(\beta)\rangle=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}\hskip-5.0ptw_{\gamma}(\beta)\hskip 1.0pt\textsf{g}(\beta)=\frac{\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}e^{-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta)}\textsf{g}(\beta)}{\sum_{\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}e^{-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta^{\prime})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{\delta}}e^{-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma,i,k}(\beta)}\textsf{g}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\sum_{\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}e^{-n\alpha\hat{R}_{n}^{\gamma}(\beta^{\prime})}e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta^{\prime})}}
