\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{\textsf{g}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right\rangle_{i,k},

(49)

(ii)
follows from Jensen’s Inequality combined with the fact that
|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(\beta)|\overset{a.s}{\leq}\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)
,
(iii)
from the fact that

\displaystyle\langle\textsf{g}(\beta)\rangle\leq\sup\nolimits_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}g(\beta),

(50)

and
(iv)
is exactly the statement of
26
for some
\textsf{C}_{1}(k,\alpha,\gamma)
. Combining (
47
) and (
48
), we conclude that

\displaystyle(a)\leq\frac{\textsf{C}_{2}k\textsf{C}_{1}(k,\alpha,\gamma)}{\sqrt{n}}=:\frac{\textsf{C}_{2}(k,\alpha,\gamma)}{\sqrt{n}}.

(51)

For the term
(b)
, we first apply
27
, which says that there exist
\textsf{D}=\textsf{D}(k,\alpha,\gamma,\tau)
and real coefficients
b_{0},\ldots,b_{\textsf{D}}
such that

\displaystyle(b)\leq\tau+\sum_{\ell=0}^{\textnormal{{D}}}|b_{\ell}|\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i,L}(\beta_0)\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\r]},

(52)

noting by definition that
\absolutevalue{b_\ell}={{\textsf{D}}\choose\ell}\leq\textsf{D}!
is bounded. Now, we define the Gaussian interpolator

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}:=\sin(t)\tilde{\mathbf{G}}+\cos(t)\mathbf{G}

with
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
as defined in
28
being an identical copy of
\mathbf{G}
. Moreover note that according to
Lemma

40
for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
g_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
such that
\|g_{\epsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\|g_{\epsilon}\|_{\rm Lipchitz}\leq 2\epsilon^{-1}
and such that

\big|g_{\epsilon}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|,|U_{i}^{T}\beta|)-\mathbb{I}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|,|U_{i}^{T}\beta|\leq L)\big|\leq\mathbb{I}\Big[|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta|\in[L-\epsilon,L]\Big]+\mathbb{I}\Big[|U_{i}^{T}\beta|\in[L-\epsilon,L]\Big].

We note that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})g_{\epsilon}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|,|U_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|)\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\right]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\right]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Big|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})\Big|\mathbb{I}\big(|U_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|\in[L-\epsilon,L])\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\Big|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})\Big|\mathbb{I}\big(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|\in[L-\epsilon,L])\right]

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Big|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})\Big|^{2}\right]^{1/2}\sqrt{P\big(|U_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|\in[L-\epsilon,L]\big)+P\big(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|\in[L-\epsilon,L]\big)}

Using
assumption

3
we know that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big|\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})\Big]\right]<\infty.

Moreover we note that

\displaystyle P\big(|U_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|\in[L-\epsilon,L]\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big(\Phi^{c}\Big(\frac{L-\epsilon-\rm{sin}(t)X_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}}{\rm{cos}(t)}\Big)\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big(\Phi^{c}\Big(\frac{L-\rm{sin}(t)X_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}}{\rm{cos}(t)}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{\rm{cos}(t)}\mathbb{E}\Big(\varphi\Big(\frac{L-\epsilon-\rm{sin}(t)X_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}}{\rm{cos}(t)}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{\rm{cos}(t)}

Similarly we can obtain that

\displaystyle P\big(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|\in[L-\epsilon,L]\big)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{\rm{sin}(t)}.

Hence we obtain that there is a constant
\textsf{C}_{3}>0
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})g_{\epsilon}(|\tilde{U}_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|,|U_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|)\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\right]

(53)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i,L}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\right]\Big|

(54)

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{C}_{3}\sqrt{\epsilon}.

(55)

Moreover we note that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_i(U_{\mathcal B_i}, \beta_0)g_\epsilon(|\tilde U_i^T\beta_0|,|U_i^T\beta_0|)\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\r]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\tilde V_i^\intercal\mathcal D_i(V_{\mathcal B_i}, \beta_0)g_\epsilon(|\tilde V_i^T\beta_0|,|V_i^T\beta_0|)\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, V_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\r]}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\mathbb{E}\Big(g_\epsilon(|\tilde V_i^T\beta_0|,|V_i^T\beta_0|)\tilde V_i\Big|(V_j)\Big)^\intercal\mathcal D_i(V_{\mathcal B_i}, \beta_0)\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, V_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\r]}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}0.

(56)

Above,
(i)
follows from the second statement of
28
coupled with
Assumption

5
, since we know that the function

\displaystyle g(X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B,G_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B):=\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}},\beta_{0})g_{\epsilon}(|\tilde{V}_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|,|V_{i}^{T}\beta_{0}|)\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big),

where
B:=(\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell})\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(\ell+1)}
is locally Lipschitz as all of its components are, and is also square integrable as

\displaystyle\sup_{B}\mathbb{E}\left[g(X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B,G_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B)^{2}\right]\leq\sup_{B}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta_{0})\right)^{2}\right]\leq\textsf{C}(k,\gamma)

(57)

by the argument in (
59
) and (
61
) of
26
. Further,
(ii)
follows from independence of
\tilde{V}_{i}
from
(V_{j})
, as

\displaystyle E[\tilde{V}_{i}V_{j}^{\intercal}]=\sin(t)\cos(t)\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}^{\intercal}]-\sin(t)\cos(t)\mathbb{E}[G_{i}G_{j}^{\intercal}]=0,

and we know that two zero-covariance Gaussians are necessarily independent of each other, combined with the fact that
x\rightarrow g_{\epsilon}(x^{T}\beta_{0},V_{i}^{T}\beta_{0})x
is a symmetric function. We may thus combine (
51
), (
52
), (
53
) and (
56
) to conclude

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\absolutevalue{\E\l[ -h'\l(f_\delta(\bU)\r)\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i,L}(\beta)\rip\r]}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\textsf{C}_{2}(k,\alpha,\gamma)}{\sqrt{n}}+\tau+\textsf{C}_{3}\sqrt{\epsilon}=\tau+\textsf{C}_{3}\sqrt{\epsilon},

(58)

As the dependence on
\epsilon
is arbitrary we get the desired result.

\jmlrQED

H.3.2
Upper Bounds on Expectations & Approximations

The next set of lemmas are used to bound various expectations that appear in the proofs of our main results. Throughout this subsection
i\leq n
designates an index. We assume that
(\mathbf{X}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j}))
and
(\mathbf{G}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{G}_{j}))
are generated under Assumptions
2
-
5
and that
\rm{Var}(\mathbf{G})=\rm{Var}(\mathbf{X})
. In addition, suppose that the block
(\mathbf{X}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j}))_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
is independent from
(\mathbf{X}_{j},y_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j}))_{j\not\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}
. We begin with a Lindeberg bound.

Lemma 25

(Lindeberg Difference Bound)
.

Let
\alpha,\delta>0
. Then there exists
\textnormal{{C}}>0
such that for each
t\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2}]
and
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\left[h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}))-h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}^{ik}))\right]^{2}\right)\leq\frac{\textnormal{{C}}k^{2}}{n}.

Proof
By the Lipschitzness of
h^{\prime}
, we first have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{h'(f_\delta(\bU)) - h'(f_\delta(\bU^{ik}))}

\displaystyle\leq\|h^{\prime}\|_{\text{Lip}}\absolutevalue{f_\delta(\bU) - f_\delta(\bU^{ik})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\textsf{C}_{1}}{n\alpha}\absolutevalue{\log\l(\frac{\sum_{\beta}\exp\l[-n\alpha\hat R_n^\gamma(\beta; \bm\eta, \bU)\r]}{\sum_{\beta}\exp\l[-n\alpha\hat R_n^\gamma(\beta; \bm\eta, \bU^{ik})\r]}\r)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\textsf{C}_{1}}{n\alpha}\left(k\alpha\log(2)+\Big\langle\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)\Big\rangle_{i,k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\textsf{C}_{1}}{n}\left(k+\Big\langle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)\Big\rangle_{i,k}\right),

where
(i)
is because
h\in\mathcal{H}
, and
(ii)
is via Jensen’s inequality on the natural logarithm and the fact that
\ell(a,0)=\log(2)
for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}))-h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}^{ik}))\right]^{2}\leq\frac{\textsf{C}_{1}}{n^{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)\Big\rangle_{i,k}^{2}+k\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)\Big\rangle_{i,k}+k^{2}\right).

From here, we can first notice that

\displaystyle\log(1+ e^{-x})\leq|x|+1\quad\ \ \ \text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

and thus, using
\absolutevalue{\eta_j}=1,\omega_{j}\leq 1
, and Cauchy-Schwarz, we can say that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\left(\absolutevalue{\eta_jU_j^\intercal\beta}+1\right)

\displaystyle\leq k+\|\beta\|\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\|U_{j}\|,

and so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)\Big\rangle_{i,k}

\displaystyle\leq k+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\|\beta\|\ \|U_{j}\|\Big\rangle_{i,k}

\displaystyle\leq k+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|U_{j}\|\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\delta}}\|\beta\|\right]

\displaystyle\leq k+\textsf{L}\sqrt{p}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|U_{j}\|\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}k+\textsf{L}\sqrt{p}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}[U_{jk}^{2}]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}k+\textsf{L}k\sqrt{p}\sup_{j}\sqrt{\text{Tr}(\Sigma_{j})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\textsf{C}_{2}k\sqrt{p},

where
(i)
is via Jensen’s Inequality,
(ii)
is because

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[U_{jk}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sin^{2}(t)X_{jk}^{2}+\cos^{2}(t)G_{jk}^{2}+\sin(t)\cos(t)X_{jk}G_{jk}\right]=(\Sigma_{j})_{k,k}\Big(\sin^{2}(t)+\cos^{2}(t)\Big)=(\Sigma_{j})_{k,k},

and
(iii)
is via
44
and the fact that

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(\Sigma_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\langle\Sigma_{j}e_{i},e_{i}\rangle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{p}\|\Sigma_{j}\|_{\text{op}}\leq p\|\Sigma_{j}\|_{\text{op}}=O(1),

where the last inequality comes from the scaling of
\mathbf{U}
.
Using an identical argument and the fact that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)\Big\rangle_{i,k}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)\Big)^{2}\Big\rangle_{i,k},

we similarly obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\langle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)\Big\rangle_{i,k}^{2}\leq\textsf{C}_{3}k^{2}p,

and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\left[h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}))-h^{\prime}(f_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}^{ik}))\right]^{2}\right)\leq\frac{\textsf{C}_{1}}{n^{2}}\left(\textsf{C}_{3}k^{2}p+\textsf{C}_{2}k^{2}\sqrt{p}+k^{2}\right)\leq\frac{\textsf{C}k^{2}}{n}

for
n
sufficiently large.

\jmlrQED

Lemma 26

(Second Moment Bound)
.

There exists
\textnormal{{C}}(k,\alpha,\gamma)>0
such that for every
t\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
and
i=1,\ldots,n
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}}\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)^{2}\right]\leq\textnormal{{C}}(k,\alpha,\gamma).

Proof
Fix some
\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}
. We may bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\right)^{4}\right]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\frac{e^{-\alpha\sum_{j}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)^{4}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\right)^{4}\right]^{1/2}\Big\langle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4\alpha\sum_{j}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\right]\Big\rangle_{i,k}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\right)^{4}\right]^{1/2}}_{(a)}\underbrace{\left(\sup_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4\alpha\sum_{j}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\right]\right)^{1/2}}_{(b)},

where
(i)
is via Cauchy-Schwarz, and
(ii)
is from the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}[\ \cdot\ ]
and
\langle\ \cdot\ \rangle_{i,k}
commute due to
w_{\gamma}^{i,k}(\beta)
only being a function of
(U_{j})_{j\notin\mathcal{B}_{i}}
, and from applying Jensen’s Inequality to the convex function
x^{-4}
, using the fact that

\displaystyle\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)\geq 0\implies\exp\left(-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)\right)\leq 1.

Also in
(ii)
, note that the conditional expectation has disappeared due to block dependence. For the term
(a)
, we can notice that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\eta_i\omega_i\sigma_{i\beta}\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta+ \sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\sigma_{j\beta}\eta_j'a_{ji}U_j^\intercal\beta\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta^*}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta}+\frac{3}{\gamma}\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta^*}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\absolutevalue{U_j^\intercal\beta},

(59)

where
(i)
is from
\eta_{i},\omega_{i},\sigma_{i\beta},a_{ji}\leq 1
and
21
bounding
\absolutevalue{\eta_j'}
. From here, we know that since the rows of
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{G}
are sub-Gaussian by
Assumption

3
, so must those of
\mathbf{U}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}
be as well, since

\displaystyle\|U_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}}=\|\sin(t)X_{i}+\cos(t)G_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\absolutevalue{\sin(t)}\|X_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}}+\absolutevalue{\cos(t)}\|G_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}\textsf{K}_{X}}{\sqrt{n}},

and similarly for
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}
. This further implies that for any
\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
we have

\displaystyle\|U_{i}^{\intercal}\beta\|_{\psi_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}\textsf{K}_{X}\|\beta\|}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}\textsf{K}_{X}\textsf{L}\sqrt{p}}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{C}_{1}

(60)

for
n
sufficiently large, and again this holds for
\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\beta
as well. We conclude by a multinomial expansion that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\right)^{4}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{4}{4\choose\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta}^{4-\ell}\frac{3^{\ell}}{\gamma^{\ell}}\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta^*}^{\ell}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\absolutevalue{U_j^\intercal\beta}\right)^{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{486}{\gamma^{4}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta}^{4-\ell}\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta^*}^{\ell}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\absolutevalue{U_j^\intercal\beta}\right)^{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{162}{\gamma^{4}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta}^{12-3\ell}+\absolutevalue{\tilde U_i^\intercal\beta^*}^{3\ell}+\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\absolutevalue{U_j^\intercal\beta}\right)^{3\ell}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{\leq}}\frac{162}{\gamma^{4}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{4}l\left(\textsf{C}_{2}\sqrt{12-3\ell}\right)^{12-3\ell}+\left(\textsf{C}_{3}\sqrt{3\ell}\right)^{3\ell}+\left(\textsf{C}_{4}k\sqrt{3\ell}\right)^{3\ell}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(v)}}{{\leq}}\textsf{C}_{5}\gamma^{-4}k^{12},

(61)

where
(i)
is from (
59
) and binomial expansion,
(ii)
is because
\ell\leq 4
and
\max_{\ell}{4\choose\ell}=6
,
(iii)
is the AM-GM Inequality for
n=3
,
(iv)
is from Proposition 2.5.2 in
Vershynin (2018)
on equivalent properties of sub-Gaussian random variables, and
(v)
is from the fact that

3\ell\vee(12-3\ell)\leq 12\quad\text{ for all }\quad 0\leq\ell\leq 4.

For the term
(b)
, we once again use that
|\omega_{i}|\leq 1
and
\log(1+e^{-x})\leq|x|+1
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
to say that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4\alpha\sum_{j}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4\alpha\sum_{j}|U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta|+1}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}e^{4k\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4\alpha\sum_{j}|U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta|}\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{4k\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}e^{4\alpha|U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta|}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}e^{4k\alpha}\left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4k\alpha|U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta|}\right]\right)^{1/k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}e^{4k\alpha(\mu+1)}\left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{4k\alpha(|U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta|-\mu)}\right]\right)^{1/k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{\leq}}e^{4k\alpha(\mu+1)}e^{\textsf{C}_{1}k^{2}\textsf{K}_{X}^{2}\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\textsf{C}_{2}k^{2}\alpha^{2}},

(62)

where
(i)
is via
\absolutevalue{\mathcal B_i}=k
,
(ii)
is via Hölder’s Inequality,
(iii)
is from adding and subtracting
\mu:=\mathbb{E}[|U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta|]
in the exponent, which satisfies

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[(U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)^{2}\right]}=\sqrt{\beta^{\intercal}\Sigma_{j}\beta}\leq\|\beta\|\ \|\Sigma_{j}\|_{\text{op}}^{1/2}\leq\frac{\textsf{C}\textsf{K}_{X}\textsf{L}\sqrt{p}}{\sqrt{n}}\leq\textsf{C}_{3}

for
n
sufficiently large, by Jensen’s Inequality and
44
, and
(iv)
is via sub-Gaussianity of the centered version of
U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta
, which is sub-Gaussian by Lemma 2.6.8 of
Vershynin (2018)
, and thus satisfies Condition
(v)
of Proposition 2.5.2 of the same text. Since this holds for all
\beta\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
, we conclude by combining (
61
) and (
62
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}}\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)^{2}\right]\leq\textsf{C}_{5}\gamma^{-2}k^{6}\exp\left(\textsf{C}_{2}k^{2}\alpha^{2}\right)=:\textnormal{{C}}(k,\alpha,\gamma).

\jmlrQED

The following lemma employs a technique developed in
Montanari and Saeed (2022)
, which will allow us to convert a complicated term involving the inverse function
1/x
into one involving a polynomial that is much more straightforward to control.

Lemma 27

(Polynomial Approximation)
.

Let
\alpha,\delta,\gamma,\tau>0
. Then there exists
\textnormal{{D}}=\textnormal{{D}}(k,\alpha,\gamma,\tau)
and coefficients
b_{0},\ldots,b_{\textnormal{{D}}}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\E\l[h'(f_\delta(\bU^{ik}))\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)\rip\r]}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\leq\tau+\sum_{\ell=0}^{\textnormal{{D}}}|b_{\ell}|\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_i(\beta_0)\exp\Big(-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j \ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta_r)\Big)\r]}.

Proof
Since
\mathbf{U}^{ik}
was constructed to be independent of
U_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}
and
\tilde{U}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}
, we first expand this quantity as

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\E\l[h'(f_\delta(\bU^{ik})\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)\rip\r]}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[\E_{(i, k)}\l[h'(f_\delta(\bU^{ik})\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)\rip\r]\r]}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[h'(f_\delta(\bU^{ik})\E_{(i, k)}\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)\rip\r]}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\leq\|h^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\cdot\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\lip\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)\rip}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\l\lip\frac{\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta)}}{\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta)}\rip_{i, k}}\r\rip_{i, k}}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\l\lip\E_{(i, k)}\l(\frac{\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta)}}{\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta)}\rip_{i, k}}\r)\r\rip_{i, k}}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(v)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sup_{\beta_{0}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}}\underbrace{\Bigg|\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left(\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta_{0})e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta_{0})}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)\Bigg|}_{(a)}\Bigg],

(63)

where
(i)
is via the law of total expectation,
(ii)
is by the independence mentioned above,
(iii)
is by definition of
h\in\mathcal{H}
and the ability to rewrite
\langle\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta)\rangle
as done previously in (
49
),
(iv)
is because
\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}[\ \cdot\ ]
and
\langle\ \cdot\ \rangle_{i,k}
commute with each other, and
(v)
is the same as in (
50
).

Now, we will approximate the inverse function
x^{-1}
by a polynomial by defining the functions

\displaystyle Q_{\textnormal{{D}}}(x):=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\textnormal{{D}}}(1-x)^{\ell}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\textnormal{{D}}}b_{\ell}x^{\ell},\quad\quad R_{\textnormal{{D}}}(x):=\frac{1}{x}-Q_{\textnormal{{D}}}(x)

for some degree
D
and
x\in(0,1]
. Recall from
26
that there exists
\textnormal{{C}}(k,\alpha,\gamma)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}}\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\frac{\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta_{0})e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta_{0})}}{\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}}\right)^{2}\right]\leq\textnormal{{C}}(k,\alpha,\gamma).

(64)

Thus we may choose the degree of our polynomial to be the exact
\textsf{D}=\textsf{D}\left(k,\alpha,\tau^{2}/\textnormal{{C}}(k,\alpha,\gamma)\right)
such that, by
43
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[R_{\textnormal{{D}}}\left(\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell(\eta_{j},U_{j}^{\intercal}\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}\right)^{2}\right]<\frac{\tau^{2}}{\textnormal{{C}}(k,\alpha,\gamma)}.

(65)

We conclude that

\displaystyle(a)

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_0)}Q_{\textsf D}\l(\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta)}\rip_{i, k}\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_0)}R_{\textsf D}\l(\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta)}\rip_{i, k}\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_0)}Q_{\textsf D}\l(\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta)}\rip_{i, k}\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[\left(\tilde{U}_{i}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\beta_{0})e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta_{0})}\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{(i,k)}\left[R_{\textsf{D}}\left(\langle e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\omega_{j}\ell_{j}(\beta)}\rangle_{i,k}\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_0)}Q_{\textsf D}\l(\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta)}\rip_{i, k}\r)\r]}+\tau,

(66)

where (i) is via the Triangle Inequality, (ii) via Cauchy-Schwarz, and
(iii)
and (
64
) with (
65
). To finish, we rewrite the first term in (
66
) like so: recall that if
X_{1},\ldots,X_{\ell}
are
\ell
i.i.d. random variables with the same distribution as some random variable
X
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[e^{X}]^{\ell}=\mathbb{E}[e^{X_{1}}]\cdots\mathbb{E}[e^{X_{\ell}}]=\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{E}[e^{X_{r}}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}e^{X_{r}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}X_{r}}\right],

which means that we can say

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_{i}(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_0)}Q_{\textsf D}\l(\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta)}\rip_{i, k}\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\hskip-160.0pt=\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_i(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_0)}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\textsf D}b_\ell\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta)}\rip_{i, k}^\ell\r]}

\displaystyle\hskip-160.0pt\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{\textsf{D}}\absolutevalue{b_\ell}\absolutevalue{\E_{(i, k)}\l[\tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_i(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_0)}\lip e^{-\alpha\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell(\eta_j, U_j^\intercal\beta)}\rip_{i, k}^\ell\r]}

\displaystyle\hskip-160.0pt\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{\textsf{D}}\absolutevalue{b_\ell}\absolutevalue{\l\lip\E_{(i, k)}\l[ \tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_i(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_r)}\r]\r\rip_{i, k, \ell}}

\displaystyle\hskip-160.0pt\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{\textsf{D}}\absolutevalue{b_\ell}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[ \tilde U_i^\intercal\mathcal D_i(\beta_0)e^{-\alpha\sum_{r=0}^\ell\sum_{j\in\mathcal B_i}\omega_j\ell_j(\beta_r)}\r]},

(67)

where the final expectation is unconditional due to independence, and
\langle\ \cdot\ \rangle_{i,k,\ell}
represents the
\ell
-dimensional joint expectation with marginals following
\langle\ \cdot\ \rangle_{i,k}
. Combining (
66
) and (
67
) with the supremum over
\beta_{0}
in (
63
), we conclude the result.

\jmlrQED

The following lemma allows us to convert the statement about Gaussian approximation from
Assumption

5
, which involves
k
terms, to one that involves arbitrarily many, which will be important when combined with the polynomial derived from the previous lemma.

Lemma 28

(
\bm{k}
-to-many Betas)
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{2\ell k}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then

1.

If
g
is bounded Lipschitz and

\displaystyle K\;\coloneqq\;\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in\mathcal{F}\\
\theta\in\mathcal{S}^{k-1}\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})\\
\in\mathcal{S}_{p}^{k}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E f\l(\sum_{i=1}^k\theta_iX_i^\intercal\beta_i\r) - \E f\l(\sum_{i=1}^k\theta_iG_i^\intercal\beta_i\r) }\;\leq\;1\;,

then there exists some
\textnormal{{C}}_{k,l}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}B=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell})\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(X_{\mathcal B_i}B, \tilde G_{\mathcal B_i}B) - g( G_{\mathcal B_i}B, \tilde G_{\mathcal B_i}B)\r]}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\leq\textnormal{{C}}_{k,l}\,\Big(\|g\|_{\rm Lip}+\|g\|_{\infty}\,\big(\mathbb{E}[\|X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}+\|G_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}+2\|\tilde{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}]+1\big)\Big)\,K^{\frac{1}{8kl-4}}

where
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
is an independent copy of
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathcal{F}
is as in
Assumption

5
.

2.

If
g
is locally Lipschitz & square-integrable and
Assumption

5
holds, then

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}B=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell})\\
\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(X_{\mathcal B_i}B, \tilde G_{\mathcal B_i}B) - g( G_{\mathcal B_i}B, \tilde G_{\mathcal B_i}B)\r]}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Proof
To prove the first statement, let
g:\mathbb{R}^{2\ell k}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded Lipschitz function. For notational simplicity, we may assume WLOG that the block
\mathcal{B}_{i}
begins at index
i
, meaning

\displaystyle X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\rule[2.15277pt]{10.76385pt}{0.5pt}&X_{i}&\rule[2.15277pt]{10.76385pt}{0.5pt}\\
\ &\vdots&\ \\
\rule[2.15277pt]{10.76385pt}{0.5pt}&X_{i+k-1}&\rule[2.15277pt]{10.76385pt}{0.5pt}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times p},\quad\quad B:=\begin{pmatrix}|&\ &|\\
\beta_{1}&\cdots&\beta_{\ell}\\
|&\ &|\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{\tilde{S}}^{\ell}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p\times\ell}.

Let us define the following quantities:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}:=B^{\intercal}\otimes I_{2k}\in\mathbb{R}^{2k\ell\times 2kp}\ \ \ \ \ \ \bm{u}:=\textsf{vec}{X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\choose\tilde{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}},\ \bm{v}:=\textsf{vec}{G_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}\choose\tilde{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2kp\times 1},

where
\textsf{vec}(A)
is the vectorized version of a matrix
A
. This allows us to say that

\displaystyle g(X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B,\tilde{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B)-g(G_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B,\tilde{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B)=g(\mathcal{M}\bm{u})-g(\mathcal{M}\bm{v}).

Now, let
\sigma>0
and define
\bm{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{2k\ell})
independent of all other quantities. Then by the Triangle Inequality,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(\mathcal M\bm{u}) - g(\mathcal M\bm{v})\r]}

\displaystyle\leq\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(\mathcal M\bm{u}) - g(\mathcal M\bm{u} + \bm Z)\r]}+\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(\mathcal M\bm{v}) - g(\mathcal M\bm{v} + \bm Z)\r]}

(68)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(\mathcal M\bm{u} + \bm Z) - g(\mathcal M\bm{v} + \bm Z)\r]}.

(69)

The two quantities in (
68
) are easily bounded, as

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(\mathcal M\bm{u}) - g(\mathcal M\bm{u} + \bm Z)\r]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{g(\mathcal M\bm{u}) - g(\mathcal M\bm{u} + \bm Z)}

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{\text{Lip}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\|\bm{Z}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{\text{Lip}}\cdot\sigma\sqrt{2k\ell},

(70)

and similarly for
\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(\mathcal M\bm{v}) - g(\mathcal M\bm{v} + \bm Z)\r]}
.
To control the quantity in (
69
), for
R>0
we consider the functions

\displaystyle g_{R}(y)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;(g(y)-\bar{g}(y))\,\mathbb{I}_{\{\|y\|_{\infty}\leq R\}}\;,

\displaystyle\bar{g}(y)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\msum_{r\leq 2k,r^{\prime}\leq l}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{g(y^{(r,r')}(R)) -g(y^{(r,r')}(-R))}{2R}$}}y_{r,r^{\prime}}\bm{e}_{r,r^{\prime}}\;,

where
y^{(r,r^{\prime})}(R)\in\mathbb{R}^{2kl}
is a copy of
y
with the
(r,r^{\prime})
-th coordinate replaced by
R
,
y_{r,r^{\prime}}
is the
(r,r^{\prime})
-th coordinate of
y
and
\bm{e}_{r,r^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2kl}
is the standard Euclidean basis vector with
1
at the position
(r,r^{\prime})
. The support of the function
g_{R}
is contained within the hyperrectangle
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2kl}\,|\,\|y\|_{\infty}\leq R\}
. Moreover,
g_{R}
is continuous inside the hyperrectangle and by the choice of
\bar{g}
,
g_{R}
agrees on the boundary of the hyperrectangle. This allows us to extend
g_{R}
to a continuous and
\|g\|_{\text{Lip}}
-Lipschitz function
\tilde{g}_{R}
that is
2R
-periodic in each coordinate. By construction

\displaystyle\;\big|\mathbb{E}[g(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})]-\mathbb{E}[\tilde{g}_{R}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})]\big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\big|\mathbb{E}[g(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})\mathbb{I}_{\{\|\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z}\|_{\infty}\leq R\}}]\big|+\big|\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})\,\mathbb{I}_{\{\|\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z}\|_{\infty}\leq R\}}]\big|

\displaystyle\qquad+\big|\mathbb{E}[\tilde{g}_{R}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})\,\mathbb{I}_{\{\|\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z}\|_{\infty}>R\}}]\big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\|g\|_{\infty}\,\mathbb{P}(\|\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z}\|_{\infty}>R)+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2kl \, \| g \|_\infty}{R}$}}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z}\|_{\infty}]+(1+2kl)\,\|g\|_{\infty}\,\mathbb{P}(\|\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z}\|_{\infty}>R)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{( 2 + 4kl) \| g \|_\infty\, \mean[ \| \cM\bu+ \bZ\|_\infty] }{R}$}}\;,

(71)

and the same bound holds with
\bm{u}
replaced by
\bm{v}
. We can now approximate
\tilde{g}_{R}
coordinate-wise by a truncated Fourier series. Since
\tilde{g}_{R}
is continuous and
\|g\|_{\text{Lip}}
-Lipschitz, applying a well-established result on uniform convergence of Fourier series to each coordinate (see e.g.
Jackson (1930)
;
Alimov et al. (1992)
) says that there exists some absolute constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{g}_{R}-\tilde{g}_{R}^{(N)}\|_{\infty}\;\leq\;C^{\prime}\|g\|_{\text{Lip}}\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\log N}{N}$}}\;,

where the truncated Fourier series is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{R}^{(N)}(y)\;\coloneqq\;\,\msum_{w\in\{-N,\ldots,N\}^{2kl}}\,e^{i\frac{\pi w^{\intercal}y}{R}}\,\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{(2R)^{2kl}}$}}\mint_{[0,2R]^{2kl}}\tilde{g}_{R}(t)e^{-i\frac{\pi w^{\intercal}t}{R}}dt\Big)\;.

This implies that

\displaystyle\;\big|\mathbb{E}\big[g(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathcal{M}\bm{v}+\mathbf{Z})\big]\big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\big|\mathbb{E}\big[g(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{g}_{R}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})\big]\big|+\big|\mathbb{E}\big[g(\mathcal{M}\bm{v}+\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{g}_{R}(\mathcal{M}\bm{v}+\mathbf{Z})\big]\big|

\displaystyle\qquad+\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{g}_{R}^{(N)}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})\big]\big|+\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}(\mathcal{M}\bm{v}+\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{g}_{R}^{(N)}(\mathcal{M}\bm{v}+\mathbf{Z})\big]\big|

\displaystyle\qquad+(\star)

\displaystyle\;\overset{\eqref{eq:hyperrectangle:bound}}{\leq}\;\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{( 2 + 4kl) \, \| g \|_\infty\, \mean[ \| \cM\bu+ \bZ\|_\infty+ \| \cM\bv+ \bZ\|_\infty] }{R}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 C' \| g \|_{\text{Lip}} \, \log N}{N}$}}+(\star)\;,

(72)

where

\displaystyle(\star)\;\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{R}^{(N)}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{g}_{R}^{(N)}(\mathcal{M}\bm{v}+\mathbf{Z})\big]\big|

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\bigg|\sum_{w\in\{-N,\ldots,N\}^{2kl}}\Big(\mathbb{E}[e^{i\frac{\pi w^{\intercal}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u}+\mathbf{Z})}{R}}-e^{i\frac{\pi w^{\intercal}(\mathcal{M}\bm{v}+\mathbf{Z})}{R}}]\Big)\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{(2R)^{2kl}}$}}\mint_{[0,2R]^{2kl}}\tilde{g}_{R}(t)e^{-i\frac{\pi w^{\intercal}t}{R}}dt\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\bigg|\sum_{w\in\{-N,\ldots,N\}^{2kl}}\Big(\mathbb{E}[e^{i\frac{\pi w^{\intercal}(\mathcal{M}\bm{u})}{R}}-e^{i\frac{\pi w^{\intercal}(\mathcal{M}\bm{v})}{R}}]\Big)e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}\|w\|^{2}}{2R^{2}}}\Big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{(2R)^{2kl}}$}}\mint_{[0,2R]^{2kl}}\tilde{g}_{R}(t)e^{-i\frac{\pi w^{\intercal}t}{R}}dt\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\|g\|_{\infty}\,\msum_{w\in\{-N,\ldots,N\}^{2kl}}\big|\psi_{\mathcal{M}u}\big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi w}{R}$}}\big)-\psi_{\mathcal{M}v}\big(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi w}{R}$}}\big)\big|\,e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}\|w\|^{2}}{2R^{2}}}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\|g\|_{\infty}\,\msum_{w\in\{-\frac{N}{R},\ldots,\frac{N}{R}\}^{2kl}}\big|\psi_{\mathcal{M}u}(\pi w)-\psi_{\mathcal{M}v}(\pi w)\big|\,e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}\|w\|^{2}}{2}}\;.

(73)

To control the difference of characteristic functions, let
t\in\mathbb{R}^{2k\ell}
, and decompose it as
t=(s,\tilde{s})
for
s,\tilde{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\ell}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\psi_{\mathcal M\bm{u}}(t) - \psi_{\mathcal M\bm{v}}(t)}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left(e^{i\textsf{vec}(X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B)^{\intercal}s}\right)\mathbb{E}\left(e^{i\textsf{vec}({\tilde{G}}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B)^{\intercal}\tilde{s}}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left(e^{i\textsf{vec}(G_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B)^{\intercal}s}\right)\mathbb{E}\left(e^{i\textsf{vec}({\tilde{G}}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B)^{\intercal}\tilde{s}}\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}2\absolutevalue{\E\l(e^{i\textsf{vec}(X_{\mathcal B_i}B)^\intercal s}\r) - \E\l(e^{i\textsf{vec}(G_{\mathcal B_i}B)^\intercal s}\r)},

(74)

where
(i)
is because the characteristic function factors due to
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\indep(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{G})
, and
(ii)
is because the characteristic function always has modulus in
[0,1]
, and
(x-y)^{2}\leq 2|x-y|
for
x,y\in[0,1]
. From here, let us now decompose our vector
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k\ell}
into
k
subvectors by defining

\displaystyle s_{r}:=s_{r(\ell-1)+1:r\ell}=\left(s_{r(\ell-1)+1},\ldots,s_{r\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}

for each
r=1,\ldots,k
. Then, we expand

\displaystyle\textsf{vec}(X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B)^{\intercal}s

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{k}(X_{r+i-1}^{\intercal}\beta_{1},\ldots,X_{r+i-1}^{\intercal}\beta_{\ell})^{\intercal}s_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{k}X_{r+i-1}^{\intercal}(Bs_{r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{k}X_{r+i-1}^{\intercal}\sum_{t=1}^{\ell}s_{rt}\beta_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{k}\|s_{r}\|_{1}X_{r+i-1}^{\intercal}\sum_{t=1}^{\ell}\frac{|s_{rt}|}{\|s_{r}\|_{1}}\text{sgn}(s_{rt})\beta_{t}

\displaystyle=:\sum_{r=1}^{k}\|s_{r}\|_{1}X_{r+i-1}^{\intercal}\nu_{r},

(75)

where each
\nu_{r}:=\sum_{t=1}^{\ell}\frac{|s_{rt}|}{\|s_{r}\|_{1}}\text{sgn}(s_{rt})\beta_{t}
is in
\mathcal{S}_{p}
, since if we define

\displaystyle(\beta_{\rm{mix}})_{rt}:=\text{sgn}(s_{rt})\beta_{t}\in\mathcal{S}_{p},\ \ \ \ \lambda_{rt}:=\frac{|s_{rt}|}{\|s_{r}\|_{1}}\in[0,1],

then we know that since
\mathcal{S}_{p}
is symmetric and convex and
\sum_{t}\lambda_{rt}=1
, and since
\mathcal{S}_{p}
contains the convex closure of
\tilde{S}
, it must be that

\displaystyle\nu_{r}=\sum_{t=1}^{\ell}\frac{|s_{rt}|}{\|s_{r}\|_{1}}\text{sgn}(s_{rt})\beta_{t}=\lambda_{r1}\tilde{\beta}_{r1}+\ldots+\lambda_{r\ell}\tilde{\beta}_{r\ell}\in\mathcal{S}_{p}.

Thus we conclude that

\displaystyle\sup_{B}\absolutevalue{\psi_{\mathcal M\bm{u}}(t) - \psi_{\mathcal M\bm{v}}(t)}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}2\sup_{B}\absolutevalue{\E\l(e^{i\sum_r\|s_r\|_1X_{r+i-1}^\intercal\beta_r}\r) - \E\l(e^{i\sum_r\|s_r\|_1G_{r+i-1}^\intercal\beta_r}\r)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{B}\absolutevalue{\E\l(e^{{i}{\sqrt{\sum_{r}\|s_r\|_1^2}}\sum_r\frac{\|s_r\|_1X_{r+i-1}^\intercal\beta_r}{{\sqrt{\sum_{r}\|s_r\|_1^2}}}}\r) - \E\l(e^{{i}{\sqrt{\sum_{r}\|s_r\|_1^2}}\sum_r\frac{\|s_r\|_1G_{r+i-1}^\intercal\beta_r}{{\sqrt{\sum_{r}\|s_r\|_1^2}}}}\r)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{S}^{k-1}}\sup_{B}\absolutevalue{\E\l(e^{{i}{\sqrt{\sum_{r}\|s_r\|_1^2}}\sum_r \theta_rX_{r+i-1}^\intercal\beta_r}\r) - \E\l(e^{{i}{\sqrt{\sum_{r}\|s_r\|_1^2}}\sum_r \theta_rG_{r+i-1}^\intercal\beta_r}\r)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}2\sqrt{\sum_{r}\|s_{r}\|_{1}^{2}}\underbrace{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in\mathcal{F}\\
\theta\in\mathcal{S}^{k-1}\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})\\
\in\mathcal{S}_{p}^{k}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\E f\l(\sum_{j=1}^k\theta_jX_{j+k-1}^\intercal\beta_j\r) - \E f\l(\sum_{j=1}^k\theta_jG_{j+k-1}^\intercal\beta_j\r) }}_{\textsf{=}K}.

Here,
(i)
is via (
74
) and (
75
), and
(ii)
is because the map
x\mapsto c^{-1}e^{icx}
is 1-Lipschitz and bounded by
c^{-1}
.
Now let
w_{r,r^{\prime}}
be the
r(l-1)+r^{\prime}
-th coordinate of
w
, where
1\leq r\leq 2k
and
1\leq r^{\prime}\leq l
.
Plugging the above bound into (
73
), we obtain

\displaystyle(\star)\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\|g\|_{\infty}\,\sqrt{2K}\,\msum_{w\in\{-\frac{N}{R},\ldots,\frac{N}{R}\}^{2kl}}\,\Big(\msum_{r=1}^{k}\Big(\msum_{r^{\prime}=1}^{l}|\pi w_{r,r^{\prime}}|\Big)^{2}\Big)^{1/4}\,e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}\|w\|^{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\|g\|_{\infty}\,\sqrt{2K}\,(kl^{2})^{1/4}\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi N}{R}$}}\,\msum_{w\in\{-\frac{N}{R},\ldots,\frac{N}{R}\}^{2kl}}\,e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}\|w\|^{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\|g\|_{\infty}\,\sqrt{2K}\,(kl^{2})^{1/4}\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi N}{R}$}}\,\Big(1+2R\msum_{w^{\prime}\in\{1,\frac{1}{R},\ldots,\frac{N}{R}\}}\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{1}{R}$}}\,e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}(w^{\prime})^{2}}{2}}\Big)^{2kl}

\displaystyle\;\leq

\displaystyle\;\|g\|_{\infty}\,\sqrt{2K}\,(kl^{2})^{1/4}\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi N}{R}$}}\,\Big(1+2R\mint_{0}^{\infty}\,e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}(w^{\prime})^{2}}{2}}\,dw^{\prime}\Big)^{2kl}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;\|g\|_{\infty}\,\sqrt{2K}\,(kl^{2})^{1/4}\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi N}{R}$}}\,\Big(1+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R \sqrt{2}}{{\sigma}}$}}\Big)^{2kl}\;.

Combining this bound with (
70
) and (
72
) by the triangle inequality, we get

\displaystyle\;\big|\mathbb{E}\big[g(\mathcal{M}\bm{u})-g(\mathcal{M}\bm{v})\big]\big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\|g\|_{\text{Lip}}\sigma\sqrt{2kl}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{( 2 + 4kl) \, \| g \|_\infty\, \mean[ \| \cM\bu+ \bZ\|_\infty+ \| \cM\bv+ \bZ\|_\infty] }{R}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 \| g \|_{\text{Lip}} C' \log N}{N}$}}

\displaystyle\qquad\;+\|g\|_{\infty}\,\sqrt{2K}\,(kl^{2})^{1/4}\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi N}{R}$}}\,\Big(1+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R \sqrt{2}}{{\sigma}}$}}\Big)^{2kl}

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\|g\|_{\text{Lip}}\sigma\sqrt{2kl}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{( 2 + 4kl) \, \| g \|_\infty\, \big( \mean[ \| \cM\bu\|_\infty+ \| \cM\bv\|_\infty] + 2kl \sigma\big) }{R}$}}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 \| g \|_{\text{Lip}} C' \log N}{N}$}}

\displaystyle\qquad\;+\|g\|_{\infty}\,\sqrt{2K}\,(kl^{2})^{1/4}\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi N}{R}$}}\,\Big(1+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{R \sqrt{2}}{{\sigma}}$}}\Big)^{2kl}\;,

where we have used the Markov inequality and a union bound. Now choose

\displaystyle\sigma\;=\;K^{\frac{1}{8kl-4}}\;,\qquad N\;=\;R\;=\;\lfloor K^{-\frac{1}{8kl}}\rfloor\;.

We get that for some absolute constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\;\big|\mathbb{E}\big[g(\mathcal{M}\bm{u})-g(\mathcal{M}\bm{v})\big]\big|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;C\Big(\|g\|_{\rm Lip}\,\big(\sqrt{2kl}\,+1\big)+\|g\|_{\infty}(1+2kl)\big(\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{M}\bm{u}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{M}\bm{v}\|_{\infty}]+kl\big)+\|g\|_{\infty}\,3^{2kl}\,(kl^{2})^{1/4}\Big)\,K^{\frac{1}{8kl-4}}

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\textnormal{{C}}_{k,l}\,\Big(\|g\|_{\rm Lip}+\|g\|_{\infty}\,\big(\mathbb{E}[\|X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}+\|G_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}+2\|\tilde{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}]+1\big)\Big)\,K^{\frac{1}{8kl-4}}

for some
\textnormal{{C}}_{k,l}>0
. We also remark that by sub-Gaussianity, the term
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}+\|G_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}+2\|\tilde{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}B\|_{\infty}]
is
O(kl)
.

To prove the second statement of the lemma, we will sketch the outline and refer to Lemma 30 of
Montanari and Saeed (2022)
for the specific details of a similar approach. We fix
B>0
, and consider
g_{B}
, which forces
g
to be bounded Lipschitz like so:

\displaystyle g_{B}(\bm{x}):=g\left(\bm{x}\right)\mathbb{I}\left(\|\bm{x}\|\leq B\right)+g\left(B\bm{x}/\|\bm{x}\|\right)\mathbb{I}\left(\|\bm{x}\|>B\right).

We may then apply the first statement of the lemma to
g_{B}
, which under
Assumption

5
converges to zero. To bound the leftover differences of the form

\displaystyle\sup_{B}\absolutevalue{\E\l[g(\bm M\bm u) - g_B(\bm M\bm u)\r]},

we use square-integrability of
g
and the fact that

\displaystyle g(\bm{M}\bm{u})-g_{B}(\bm{M}\bm{u})\neq 0\implies\|\bm{M}\bm{u}\|>B,

which by sub-Gaussianity of all
2k\ell
components of
\bm{M}\bm{u}
occurs with probability bounded by

\textsf{C}_{1}k\ell\exp\left(-\textsf{c}_{2}B^{2}/k\ell\right)
. Sending
B\to\infty
thus concludes the result.

\jmlrQED

Appendix I
Comparing training loss with deleted blocks to the original training loss

Let
M,\tilde{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be fixed. Define new matrices
\mathbf{X}^{M},\mathbf{G}^{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}\times p}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{M}

\displaystyle:=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{M},X_{M+\tilde{m}+1},\ldots,X_{2M+\tilde{m}},X_{2M+2\tilde{m}+1},\ldots)^{\intercal}

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{M}

\displaystyle:=(G_{1},\ldots,G_{M},G_{M+\tilde{m}+1},\ldots,G_{2M+\tilde{m}},G_{2M+2\tilde{m}+1},\ldots)^{\intercal}.

We show that the training loss

\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X})
and
\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M})
are close if
M
is large.

Theorem 29

(Equivalence of losses)
.

Let
\left(X_{i},y_{i}(X_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
and
\left(G_{i},y_{i}(G_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
be generated under Assumptions
2
-
4
, where each
G_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{0},\text{\rm Var}(X_{i})\right)
. Then if
assumption

8
(i) is respected then there exists a constant
\textsf{C}_{d}
such that

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M})\right)\leq

\displaystyle\textsf{C}_{d}\frac{\max(m,\tilde{m})}{M}\sqrt{M+\tilde{m}}

(76)

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{G}^{M})\right)\leq

\displaystyle\textsf{C}_{d}\frac{\max(m,\tilde{m})}{M}\sqrt{M+\tilde{m}}

(77)

If instead
assumption

8
(ii) is respected then there exists a constant
\textsf{C}^{\prime}_{d}
such that

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M})\right)\leq

\displaystyle\textsf{C}^{\prime}_{d}\frac{\max(\mathcal{S},\tilde{m})}{M}\sqrt{M+\tilde{m}}

(78)

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{G}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{G}^{M})\right)\leq

\displaystyle\textsf{C}^{\prime}_{d}\frac{\max(\mathcal{S},\tilde{m})}{M}\sqrt{M+\tilde{m}}

(79)

Proof of
Theorem

29

We present the proof in the
m
-dependent case but the proof is identical in the other case. Similarly we show the proof only for
\mathbf{X}
as the proof is equivalent for
\mathbf{G}
.
Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be fixed. Define new matrices
\mathbf{X}^{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}\times p}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{M}

\displaystyle:=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{M},X_{M+\tilde{m}+1},\ldots,X_{2M+\tilde{m}},X_{2M+2m+1},\ldots)^{\intercal}

noting that

\displaystyle n^{\prime}\in[n-(r+1)m+1,\ n-rm]\subset\left[n\frac{M}{M+\tilde{m}}-m,\ n\frac{M}{M+\tilde{m}}+m\right]=[nq-\tilde{m},nq+\tilde{m}],

(80)

where
r:=\lfloor\frac{n}{M+\tilde{m}}\rfloor
and
q:=\frac{M}{M+\tilde{m}}
. We may also define

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\tilde{m}}

\displaystyle:=(X_{M+1},\dots,X_{M+\tilde{m}},X_{2M+\tilde{m}+1},\dots,X_{2M+2\tilde{m}},\dots)

so that every vector
X_{i}
is either in
\mathbf{X}^{M}
or
\mathbf{X}^{m}
. For simplicity we can also write these indexing sets as

\displaystyle B_{M}

\displaystyle:=\{1,\ldots,M,M+\tilde{m}+1,\ldots,2M+m,\ldots,\}

\displaystyle B_{m}

\displaystyle:=[n]\setminus B_{M}.

By a triangle inequality argument we note that

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X}),\min_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M})\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\absolutevalue{\E\l[h\l(\min_\beta\hat R_n(\beta; \bX)\r) - h\l(\min_\beta\hat R_{n'}(\beta; \bX^M)\r)\r]}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{h\l(\min_\beta\hat R_n(\beta; \bX)\r) - h\l(\min_\beta\hat R_{n'}(\beta; \bX^M)\r)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\min_\beta\hat R_n(\beta; \bX) - \min_\beta\hat R_{n'}(\beta; \bX^M)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=:}}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\hat\beta; \bX) - \hat R_{n'}(\tilde\beta; \bX^M)},

(81)

where
(i)
is via the Lipschitzness of
h
, and in
(ii)
we defined the minimizers
\hat{\beta}:=\text{argmin}_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{X})
and
\tilde{\beta}:=\text{argmin}_{\beta}\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\beta;\mathbf{X}^{M})
. We first control the difference inside the absolute value of (
81
) by noting that, by definition of being minimizers,

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{n}(\hat{\beta};\mathbf{X})-\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\tilde{\beta};\mathbf{X}^{M})

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{n}{n^{\prime}}\right)\hat{R}_{n}(\hat{\beta})+\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}\ell\left(\eta_{i},X_{i}^{\intercal}\tilde{\beta}\right)\leq\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}\ell\left(\eta_{i},X_{i}^{\intercal}\tilde{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\tilde{\beta};\mathbf{X}^{M})-\hat{R}_{n}(\hat{\beta};\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\right)\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\tilde{\beta})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}\ell\left(\eta_{i},X_{i}^{\intercal}\hat{\beta}\right)\leq\left(1-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\right)\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\tilde{\beta}),

where the terms removed are because of the fact that the risk/loss is always positive and
n>n^{\prime}
. Now, we will use the fact that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
a,b>0
, we have

\displaystyle x\leq a,\ -x\leq b\implies|x|\leq a\vee b\leq a+b

to say that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\hat\beta; \bX) - \hat R_{n'}(\tilde\beta; \bX^M)}\leq\left(1-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\right)\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\tilde{\beta})+\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}\ell\left(\eta_{i},X_{i}^{\intercal}\tilde{\beta}\right).

(82)

For the first term on the right in (
82
), we note by definition of being a minimizer that

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\right)\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(\tilde{\beta})\leq\left(1-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\right)\hat{R}_{n^{\prime}}(0)=\left(1-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\right)\log(2).

(83)

For the second term in (
82
), we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}\ell\left(\eta_{i},X_{i}^{\intercal}\tilde{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}1+|X_{i}^{\intercal}\tilde{\beta}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n-n^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sup_{\beta}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}|X_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|

\displaystyle=\frac{n-n^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sup_{\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|\mathbb{I}(i\in B_{m})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\frac{n-n^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sup_{\beta}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{i}^{\intercal}\beta|^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}(i\in B_{m})}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-n^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}+\frac{\sqrt{n-n^{\prime}}}{n^{\prime}}\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}},

(84)

where
(i)
is because

\displaystyle\log(1 + e^{-x})\leq|x|+1\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

and
(ii)
is via Cauchy-Schwarz. Combining (
83
) and (
84
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\hat\beta; \bX) - \hat R_{n'}(\tilde\beta; \bX^M)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\right)\log(2)+\frac{n-n^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}+\frac{\sqrt{n-n^{\prime}}}{n^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}\right]

(85)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\left(1-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\right)\log(2)+\frac{n-n^{\prime}}{n^{\prime}}+\frac{\sqrt{n-n^{\prime}}}{n^{\prime}}\sqrt{\textsf{C}_{L}mp},

(86)

where
(i)
is via Jensen’s Inequality with
20
. From here we can use (
21
) to note that, for these quantities involving both
n
and
n^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle n^{\prime}\in nq\pm\tilde{m}\implies\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\in q\pm\frac{\tilde{m}}{n}\implies\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}=q.

(87)

Substituting this into (
86
) and using that
q=\frac{M}{M+\tilde{m}}
, after some simplification we can conclude that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\absolutevalue{\hat R_n(\hat\beta; \bX) - \hat R_{n'}(\tilde\beta; \bX^M)}

\displaystyle\leq(1-q)\log(2)+\left(\frac{1}{q}-1\right)+\sqrt{\frac{\textsf{C}_{L}m\kappa}{q}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{q}-1}

(88)

\displaystyle\leq\textsf{C}_{2}\frac{\max(m,\tilde{m})}{M}\sqrt{M+\tilde{m}}.

(89)

\jmlrQED

Appendix J
Verifying Assumption
5
for Different Data Augmentation Schemes

J.1
Random Cropping

The data augmentation procedure that we are considering in this subsection is the random cropping method where a portion of the data is randomly set to
0
. For a vector
e:=(e_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{p}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}

we write
e\cdot x:=(e_{i}x_{i})
. Let
(E_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence of random vectors in
\{0,1\}^{p}
and define the random transformations
\phi_{i}(x)=E_{i}\cdot x
. We will prove that
Assumption

5
holds for this type of data augmentation procedure under general conditions. Let
(Z_{i})
a sequence of i.i.d vectors satisfying the following condition

(H_{\text{cropping}}(\kappa))\qquad\mathbb{E}(Z_{1})=0,~\sup_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|_{4}<\kappa/\sqrt{n.}

Define

X_{i}:=\phi_{i}(Z_{\lceil i/k\rceil}).

Lemma 30
.

Suppose that the assumption
H_{\text{cropping}}(\kappa)
holds and that the entries
(Z_{1,i})
are locally dependent and write
N_{i}
the dependency neighborhood of
Z_{1,i}
. Suppose that
(E_{1,i})
is locally dependent and write
\tilde{N}_{i}
the local dependency neighborhood of
E_{1,i}
. Then if
|N_{i}|\times|\tilde{N}_{i}|=o(n^{r/2})

Assumption

5
holds for a sequence of random Gaussian vector
(G_{i})
with covariance:

\text{cov}(G_{i,j},G_{m,l})=\begin{cases}p_{l}(\mathbb{I}(j=l)+\mathbb{I}(j\neq l)p_{j})\text{\rm Var}(Z_{1,j},Z_{1,l})~\text{if}~i=m\\
p_{j}p_{l}\text{cov}(Z_{1,j},Z_{1,l})~\text{if}~|i-m|\leq k,~i\neq m\\
0~\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
p_{j}=P(E_{1,j}=1).

Proof
Note that as the blocks
(X_{mk+1},\dots,X_{(m+1)k})
are identically distributed it is enough to prove
Assumption

5
for
m=0
. Denote
B_{i,j}=\cup_{j\in N_{i}}\tilde{N}_{j}\times[|1,k|]
. Then we remark that the sequence
(X_{i})
is locally dependent and that the dependency neighborhood of
X_{i,j}
is
B_{i,j}.
The desired result follows from
42
with
q=1
.

\jmlrQED

J.2
Noise Injection

The data augmentation procedure that we are considering in this subsection is the noise injection method where random Gaussian noise is injected to the entries. For vectors
g:=(g_{i}),x:=(x_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{p}

we write
g\cdot x:=(g_{i}+x_{i})
. Let
(n_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence of random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
n_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}/nId)
and define the random transformations
\phi_{i}(x)=n_{i}\cdot x
. We will prove that
Assumption

5
holds for this type of data augmentation procedure under general conditions. Let
(Z_{i})
be a sequence of i.i.d vectors satisfying the following condition

(H_{\text{noise}}(\kappa))~\mathbb{E}(Z_{1})=0,\qquad\sup_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|_{4}<\kappa/\sqrt{n.}

Define
X_{i}:=\phi_{i}(Z_{\lceil i/k\rceil}).

Lemma 31
.

Suppose that the assumption
H_{\text{noise}}(\kappa)
holds for an absolute constant
\kappa<\infty
and that the entries
(Z_{1,i})
are locally dependent. Write
N_{i}
the dependency neighborhood of
Z_{1,i}.
Suppose that
(n_{1,i})\sim N(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}Id)
. Then if
|N_{i}|=o(n^{r/2})
,
Assumption

5
holds for a sequence of random Gaussian vector
(G_{i})
with covariance:

\text{cov}(G_{i,j},G_{m,l})=\begin{cases}\text{cov}(Z_{1,j},Z_{1,l})+\delta_{j,l}\delta_{i,m}\sigma^{2}/n~\text{if}~i=m\\
\text{cov}(Z_{1,j},Z_{1,l})~\text{if}~|i-m|\leq k,i\neq m\\
0~\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Proof
Note that as the blocks
(X_{mk+1},\dots,X_{(m+1)k})
are identically distributed it is enough to prove
Assumption

5
for
m=0
. Denote
B_{i,j}=N_{i}\times[|1,k|]
. Then we remark that the sequence
(X_{i})
is locally dependent and that the dependency neighborhood of
X_{i,j}
is
B_{i,j}.
The desired result follows from
42
with
q=1
.

\jmlrQED

J.3
Random Sign Flipping

The data augmentation procedure that we are considering in this subsection is the random sign-flip method, where a portion of the data is has it sign randomly flipped. For a vector
e:=(e_{i})\in\{-1,1\}^{p}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}

we write
e\cdot x:=(e_{i}x_{i})
. Let
(E_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence of random vectors in
\{-1,1\}^{p}
and define the random transformations
\phi_{i}(x)=E_{i}\cdot x
. We will prove that
Assumption

5
holds for this type of data augmentation procedure under general conditions. Let
(Z_{i})
a sequence of i.i.d vectors satisfying the following condition

(H_{\text{flip}}(\kappa))\qquad\mathbb{E}(Z_{1})=0,~\sup_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|_{4}<\kappa/\sqrt{n.}

Define

X_{i}:=\phi_{i}(Z_{\lceil i/k\rceil}).

Lemma 32
.

Suppose that the assumption
H_{\text{flip}}(\kappa)
holds and that the entries
(Z_{1,i})
are locally dependent and write
N_{i}
the dependency neighborhood of
Z_{1,i}
. Suppose that
(E_{1,i})
is locally dependent and write
\tilde{N}_{i}
the local dependency neighborhood of
E_{1,i}
. Then if
|N_{i}|\times|\tilde{N}_{i}|=o(n^{r/2})

Assumption

5
holds for a sequence of random Gaussian vector
(G_{i})
with covariance:

\text{cov}(G_{i,j},G_{m,l})=\begin{cases}p^{*}_{j,l}\mathbb{E}(Z_{1,j},Z_{1,l})-(1-p^{*}_{j,l})\mathbb{E}(Z_{1,j},Z_{1,l})\quad\rm{if}~i=m\\
p_{j,l}\mathbb{E}(Z_{1,j},Z_{1,l})-(1-p_{j,l})\mathbb{E}(Z_{1,j},Z_{1,l})~\text{if}~|i-m|\leq k~i\neq m\\
0~~\text{~otherwise}\end{cases}

where
p_{j,l}=P(E_{1,j}=1)^{2}+P(E_{1,j}=-1)^{2}
and
p^{*}_{j,l}=P(E_{1,j}=1,E_{1,l}=1)+P(E_{1,j}=-1,E_{1,l}=-1).

Proof
Note that as the blocks
(X_{mk+1},\dots,X_{(m+1)k})
are identically distributed it is enough to prove
Assumption

5
for
m=0
. Denote
B_{i,j}=\cup_{j\in N_{i}}\tilde{N}_{j}\times[|1,k|]
. Then we remark that the sequence
(X_{i})
is locally dependent and that the dependency neighborhood of
X_{i,j}
is
B_{i,j}.
The desired result follows from
42
with
q=1
.

\jmlrQED

Remark 33
.

Note that we do not assume that the probability of having a sign flipped at one position is the same than at any other positions

J.4
Random Small Permutations

In this section we will show that
Assumption

5
holds for a random permutation scheme. In this goal, let
(Z_{i})
be a sequence of centered i.i.d random vectors with independent (not necessarily identically distributed) entries. We assume that the vectors
(Z_{i})
have blocks of identically distributed entries. More precisely we suppose that there is a partition
(B_{i})_{i\leq M_{n}}
of
[|p|]
in
M_{n}
subsets such that the entries
(Z_{1,i})_{i\in B_{u}}
are i.i.d for all
u\leq M_{n}
. We choose
(\pi_{i})
to be an i.i.d sequence of random permutations of
[|n|]
that preserve the partition, meaning that for all
j,k\leq n
that do not belong to the same permutation element then
P(\pi_{1}(j)=k)=0
. Define

X_{i}:=(Z_{\lceil i/k\rceil,\pi^{-1}_{i}(\ell)}).

We will show that this data augmentation scheme satisfies
Assumption

5
. In this goal we define the following condition

(H_{\text{small~permutation}}(\kappa))\qquad\mathbb{E}(Z_{1})=0,~\sup_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|_{4}<\kappa/\sqrt{n.}

Lemma 34
.

Suppose that the assumption
H_{\text{small~permutation}}(\kappa)
holds. Suppose that
\max_{i}|B_{i}|=o(n^{r/2})

Assumption

5
holds for a sequence of random Gaussian vector
(G_{i})
with covariance:

\text{cov}(G_{i,j},G_{m,l})=\begin{cases}\sum_{k\in B_{B^{-1}(j)}}p_{k}^{2}\rm{var}(Z_{1,k})~\text{if}~|i-m|\leq k\\
0~\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

where
B^{-1}(j)
denotes the unique index such that
j\in B_{B^{-1}(j)}
and where
p_{k}:=P(\pi(j)=k)

Proof
Note that as the blocks
(X_{mk+1},\dots,X_{(m+1)k})
are identically distributed it is enough to prove
Assumption

5
for
m=0
. Denote
B_{i,j}=B_{\ell}\times[|1,k|]
if
i\in P_{\ell}
. Then we remark that the sequence
(X_{i})
is locally dependent and that the dependency neighborhood of
X_{i,j}
is
B_{i,j}.
The desired result follows from
42
with
q=1
.

\jmlrQED

J.5
Random Large Permutations

In this section we will show that
Assumption

5
holds for a random permutation scheme. In this goal, let
(Z_{i})
be a sequence of centered i.i.d random vectors with independent (not necessarily identically distributed) entries. We assume that the vectors
(Z_{i})
have blocks of identically distributed entries. More precisely we suppose that there is a partition
(B_{i})_{i\leq M}
of
[|p|]
in
M
subsets such that the entries
(Z_{1,i})_{i\in B_{u}}
are i.i.d for all
u\leq M
. Contrary to the previous section we will have
M<<n
. We choose
(\pi_{i})
to be an i.i.d sequence of random permutations of
[|n|]
that preserve the partition, meaning that for all
j,k\leq n
that do not belong to the same permutation element then
P(\pi_{1}(j)=k)=0
. Moreover we assume that for all
j,k\in B_{u}
in the same partition we have
P(\pi_{1}(j)=k)=1/|B_{u}|.
Define

X_{i}:=(Z_{\lceil i/k\rceil,\pi^{-1}_{i}(\ell)}).

Lemma 35
.

Suppose that there exists an absolute constant
\kappa<\infty
such that
\max_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|_{3}\leq\kappa/\sqrt{n}
and
\max|B_{u}|/\min|B_{u}|<\infty

Assumption

5
holds for a sequence of random Gaussian vectors that are such that

\text{cov}(G_{i,j},G_{m,l})=\sum_{u}\frac{\sigma_{u}^{2}\mathbb{I}(|i-m|\leq k)}{|B_{u}|^{2}}

where
\sigma_{u}^{2}=\text{\rm Var}(Y_{1,l})
where
l\leq p
is chosen to be in
l\in B_{u}.

Proof
Note that as the blocks
(X_{mk+1},\dots,X_{(m+1)k})
are identically distributed it is enough to prove
Assumption

5
for
m=0
. For all
(\theta_{i})\in\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
and all
(\beta_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{p}^{k}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}X_{i}^{T}\beta_{i}=\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\sum_{l}X_{i,l}\beta_{i,l}=\sum_{l}Z_{1,l}\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(l)}.

Now we notice that conditionally on
(\pi_{i})
the random variables
\big(Z_{1,l}\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(l)}\big)
are independent. Moreover we observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(Z_{1,l}\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(l)}\big|(\pi_{i})\big)

\displaystyle=0

and for all
l,m\leq p
we have

\displaystyle\text{cov}\big(Z_{1,l}\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(l)},Z_{1,m}\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(l)}\big|\pi\big)

\displaystyle=\delta_{l,m}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(l)}\big)^{2}\text{\rm Var}(Z_{1,l})

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(\big|Z_{1,l}\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(l)}\big|^{3}\big|\pi\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{k}^{3}\max_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|^{3}_{3}\sqrt{\sum_{i\leq k}\beta_{i\pi_{i}(l)}^{2}}^{3}

\displaystyle\leq k^{4}Lp^{3/2-3r/2}\max_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|^{3}_{3}

where for the last inequality we used
Assumption

4
. Define

\hat{\sigma}^{2}:=\sum_{\ell\leq p}\text{\rm Var}(Z_{1,\ell})\big(\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(\ell)}\big)^{2}=\sum_{u}\sigma_{u}^{2}\sum_{\ell\in B_{u}}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(\ell)}\big)^{2}.

According to
41
we have that there exists an absolute constant
\kappa>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{f~\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big(f\big(\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}X_{i}^{T}\beta_{i}\big)\big|(\pi_{i})\Big)-\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\hat{\sigma}^{2}\big)}\Big(f(Z)|(\pi_{i})\Big)\Big|\leq\kappa^{1/3}p^{1/2-r/2}\max_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|_{3}.

(90)

Now using the definition of
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
we observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(\hat{\sigma}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u}\sigma_{u}^{2}\frac{1}{|B_{u}|^{k}}\sum_{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k}\in B_{u}}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\ell_{i}}\big)^{2}

(91)

To show that
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
converges to
\mathbb{E}(\hat{\sigma}^{2})
we will proceed by showing that for all
S\leq k
we have
\mathbb{E}(\hat{\sigma}^{2}|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S})
concentrates around
\mathbb{E}(\hat{\sigma}^{2}|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S-1})
. The desired outcome then results from a telescopic sum argument.
In this goal we first notice that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\hat{\sigma}^{2}|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S})-\mathbb{E}(\hat{\sigma}^{2}|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{u}\sigma_{u}^{2}\sum_{\ell\in B_{u}}\sum_{i\neq S}\theta_{i}\theta_{S}\mathbb{E}\Big(\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(\ell)}\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(\ell)}|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big(\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(\ell)}\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(\ell)}|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S-1}\Big)

To bound this set
I\sim\text{unif}([|n|])
and
J\sim\text{unif}(B_{B^{-1}(I)})
where we denote
B^{-1}(i)
the index
u
such that
i\in B_{u}
. Define
\pi^{\prime}_{S}=\pi_{S}\circ(I,J)
and
\pi^{\prime}_{i}=\pi_{i}
for all
i\neq S
. Define

(\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})^{2}:=\sum_{\ell\leq p}\text{\rm Var}(Y_{1,l})\big(\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\beta_{i,\pi^{\prime}_{i}(\ell)})^{2}

then we notice that
(\hat{\sigma},\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})
forms an exchangeable pair and that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(\hat{\sigma}^{2}|\pi_{1:S})-\mathbb{E}\big((\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})^{2}|\pi_{1:S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{u}\sigma_{u}^{4}\sum_{\ell\in B_{u}}\sum_{i\neq S}\theta_{i}\theta_{S}\mathbb{E}\Big(\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(\ell)}\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(\ell)}|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big(\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(\ell)}\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(\ell)}|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S-1}\Big).

Moreover we observe that

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{2}-(\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma^{2}_{B^{-1}(I)}\sum_{i\neq S}\theta_{i}\theta_{S}(\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(I)}\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(I)}-\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(I)}\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(J)})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sigma^{2}_{B^{-1}(I)}\sum_{i\neq S}\theta_{i}\theta_{S}(\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(J)}\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(J)}-\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(J)}\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(I)})

Hence using

Chatterjee (2005)
we obtain that

\displaystyle\text{\rm Var}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(\hat{\sigma}^{2}\big|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S}\big)\Big|\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{S-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big([\hat{\sigma}^{2}-(\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})^{2}]^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 4\frac{\max_{u}\sigma^{2}_{u}}{p\min_{u}|B_{u}|}\sum_{\ell\leq p}\sum_{k\in B_{B^{-1}(\ell)}}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}\theta_{S}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(\ell)}^{2}(\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(\ell)}-\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(k)})\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 8k\frac{\max_{u}\sigma^{4}_{u}}{p\min_{u}|B_{u}|}\sum_{\ell}\sum_{k\in B_{B^{-1}(\ell)}}\theta_{S}^{2}\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}^{2}\beta_{i,\pi_{i}(\ell)}^{2}(\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(\ell)}^{2}+\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(k)}^{2})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}8k\frac{\max_{u}\sigma^{4}_{u}L}{p^{r}\min_{u}|B_{u}|}\sum_{\ell}\sum_{k\in B_{B^{-1}(\ell)}}(\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(\ell)}^{2}+\beta_{S,\pi_{S}(k)}^{2})

\displaystyle\overset{}{\leq}16k\frac{\max_{u}\sigma^{4}_{u}L\max_{u}|B_{u}|}{p^{r}\min_{u}|B_{u}|}\sum_{\ell}\beta_{S,\ell}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{}{\leq}16k\frac{\max_{u}\sigma^{4}_{u}S^{2}\max_{u}|B_{u}|p^{1-r}}{\min_{u}|B_{u}|}

where (a) is a result of
Assumption

4
. Hence as every function
f\in\mathcal{F}
is Lipschitz we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{f~\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big(f\big(\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}X_{i}^{T}\beta_{i}\big)\Big)-\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\big)}\Big(f(Z)\Big)\Big|\leq\kappa p^{1/2-r/2}\max_{i}\|Z_{1,i}\|_{3}+4\sqrt{k\frac{\max_{u}\sigma^{4}_{u}L^{2}\max_{u}|B_{u}|p^{1-r}}{\min_{u}|B_{u}|}}

Finally we note that
\sum_{i\leq k}\theta_{i}G_{i}^{T}\beta_{i}\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})
and the required result is hence deduced.
\jmlrQED

Appendix K
Auxiliary Lemmas

K.1
m-dependent Bernstein

The first lemma aims to extend the classic Bernstein’s Inequality from the usual independent setting to
m
-dependence, which of course also then includes block dependence.

Lemma 36

(
m
-dependent Bernstein)
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
be centered, sub-exponential,
m
-dependent random variables, and
\textnormal{{K}}:=\sup_{i}\|Z_{i}\|_{\psi_{1}}
. Then there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that for
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\absolutevalue{\frac{1}{n}\sumi{n}Z_i}\geq t\right)\leq 4\cdot\exp\left[-\frac{cn}{m}\left(\frac{t}{\textnormal{{K}}}\wedge\frac{t^{2}}{\textnormal{{K}}^{2}}\right)\right].

Proof
Define
r:=\lfloor n/m\rfloor
, and assume without loss of generality that
r
is even. For each
j=1,\ldots,r
, let

\displaystyle Y_{j}

\displaystyle:=\hskip-8.0pt\sum_{\ell=(j-1)m+1}^{jm}\hskip-8.0ptZ_{\ell}.

Also, define
Y_{r+1}:=\sum_{\ell=rm+1}^{n}\hskip-1.0ptZ_{\ell}
. By construction, the set
(Y_{1},Y_{3},\ldots,Y_{r+1})
is independent, as is the set
(Y_{2},Y_{4},\ldots,Y_{r})
. We can then see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\absolutevalue{\frac{1}{n}\sumi{n}Z_i}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\absolutevalue{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{r+1}Y_j}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\absolutevalue{\frac 1n\sum_{j=0}^{r/2}Y_{2j+1}}\geq\frac{t}{2}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\absolutevalue{\frac 1n\sum_{j=1}^{r/2}Y_{2j}}\geq\frac{t}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\absolutevalue{\frac{1}{\frac{r}{2}+1}\sum_{j=0}^{r/2}Y_{2j+1}}\geq\frac{nt}{r+2}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\absolutevalue{\frac{1}{\frac r2}\sum_{j=1}^{r/2}Y_{2j}}\geq\frac{nt}{r}\right)

(92)

By the Triangle Inequality, we know that each
Y_{j}
is still still sub-exponential with norm

\displaystyle\|Y_{j}\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\sum_{\ell=(j-1)m+1}^{jm}\hskip-8.0pt\|Z_{\ell}\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq m\textsf{K}.

To bound the first term in (
92
), we use Corollary 2.8.3 of
Vershynin (2018)
to say that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\absolutevalue{\frac{1}{\frac{r}{2}+1}\sum_{j=0}^{r/2}Y_{2j+1}}\geq\frac{nt}{r+2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\exp\left[-c\left(\frac{r}{2}+1\right)\left(\frac{nt}{m(r+2)\textsf{K}}\wedge\frac{n^{2}t^{2}}{m^{2}(r+2)^{2}\textsf{K}^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\exp\left[-\frac{cn}{m}\left(\frac{t}{2\textsf{K}}\wedge\frac{t^{2}}{4\textsf{K}^{2}}\right)\right],

where above we used the fact that
r+2\geq\frac{n}{m}
. We conclude by noting that this inequality also holds for the second term in (
92
) by the same reasoning.

\jmlrQED

K.2
Lemmas for mixing processes

In this subsection, we present some useful lemmas to deal with
\beta
-mixing processes. The first one allows one to relate the expectation of a bounded function of mixing data to the one of independent blocks.

Lemma 37

(
Yu (1994)
(Corollary 2.7))
.

Let
K\geq 1
be an integer and let
r_{1}\leq s_{1}\leq r_{2}\leq\dots\leq s_{K}
be a sequence of integers. Let
\tilde{X}=(\tilde{X}_{i})
be a stochastic process taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Suppose that
h:\prod_{\ell\leq K}\mathbb{R}^{p|s_{\ell}-r_{\ell}|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is measurable function. Let
Q
be the distribution of
(\tilde{X}_{j})_{j\in\cup_{\ell}[r_{\ell},s_{\ell}]}
and
Q_{i}
be the marginal distribution of
(\tilde{X}_{j})_{j\in[r_{i},s_{i}]}
. Let
\beta_{\max}=\sup_{1\leq i\leq K-1}\beta\left(k_{i}\right)
, where
k_{i}=r_{i+1}-s_{i}
, and the
P
the product measure
P=\prod_{i=1}^{K}Q_{i}
. Then if
\|h\|_{\infty}
we have,

|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}[h(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}[h(Y)]|\leq(K-1)B\beta_{\max}

We adapt this lemma to hold for functions with bounded moments.

Lemma 38
.

Let
K\geq 1
be an integer and let
r_{1}\leq s_{1}\leq r_{2}\leq\dots\leq s_{K}
be a sequence of integers. Let
\tilde{X}=(\tilde{X}_{i})
be a stochastic process taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Suppose that
h:\prod_{\ell\leq K}\mathbb{R}^{p|s_{\ell}-r_{\ell}|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is measurable function. Let
Q
be the distribution of
(\tilde{X}_{j})_{j\in\cup_{\ell}[r_{\ell},s_{\ell}]}
and
Q_{i}
be the marginal distribution of
(\tilde{X}_{j})_{j\in[r_{i},s_{i}]}
. Let
\beta_{\max}=\sup_{1\leq i\leq K-1}\beta\left(k_{i}\right)
, where
k_{i}=r_{i+1}-s_{i}
, and the
P
the product measure
P=\prod_{i=1}^{K}Q_{i}
. Then, if there exists
L>1
such that
|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}[h(X)^{L}],\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}[h(Y)^{L}]<\infty
we have

|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}[{h}(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}[{h}(Y)]|\leq 2({(K-1)\beta_{\max}})^{1-1/L}\big(\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}(h(X)^{L})\big)^{1/L}+\big(\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}(h(Y)^{2})\big)^{1/L}.

Proof
Let
C>0
be a positive constant then define
\bar{h}^{C}
as the following function
\bar{h}^{C}(x)=h(x)\mathbb{I}(|h(x)|\leq C).
Now according to
37
we have that

|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}[\bar{h}^{C}[X]]-\mathbb{E}_{Y}\sim{P}[\bar{h}^{C}[Y]]|\leq(K-1)C\beta_{\max}

Now, moreover, note that

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}\Big(h(X)\mathbb{I}(|h(X)|>C)\Big)-\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}\Big(h(Y)\mathbb{I}(|h(Y)|>C)\Big)|\leq\frac{1}{C^{L-1}}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}(h(X)^{L})+\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}(h(Y)^{L})\Big\}

Hence, by triangle inequality, we obtain that

|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}[{h}(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}[{h}(Y)]|\leq(K-1)C\beta_{\max}+\frac{1}{C^{L-1}}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}(h(X)^{L})+\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}(h(Y)^{L})\Big\}.

By choosing
C:=\Big({\frac{(L-1)\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}(h(X)^{L})+\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}(h(Y)^{L})\Big\}}{(K-1)\beta_{\max}}}\Big)^{1/L}
we get the desired result.

\jmlrQED

The next lemma establishes concentration for some function of
\beta-
mixing random variables.

Lemma 39
.

Let
(\mathbf{X}_{i})
be a sequence of random variables with mixing coefficients
(\beta(i))
. Assume that there exists a process
(\mathbf{Z}_{i})
of centered independent random vectors such for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exists constants
(c_{i,j})
such that we have

\mathbf{X}_{i}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}c_{i,j}\mathbf{Z}_{j}.

Suppose that
K:=\sup_{j}\sqrt{p}\|\mathbf{Z}_{j}\|_{\psi_{2}}<\infty
and that
\rm{Var}(Z_{j})=\Sigma/p
is such that
\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma)\geq\underline{c}>0
. Moreover assume that there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\mathcal{S}:=\sum_{\ell<\infty}{\beta(\ell)}^{\frac{\epsilon}{2+\epsilon}}<\infty
. Then there exists constants
C,\tilde{C},\tilde{C}^{\prime}
such that for all
\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{p}

P\big(\big|\sum_{i\leq n}(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2}-\mathbb{E}((\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2})\big|\geq t\big)\leq 2e^{-\frac{Ct\underline{c}}{K\mathcal{S}\|\beta\|^{2}_{2}/p}\min\Big(\frac{t\underline{c}}{\tilde{C}{K}n\|\beta\|^{2}/p},\frac{1}{\tilde{C}^{\prime}}\Big)}.

Let
(\mathbf{G}_{i})
be a sequence of centered Gaussian random variables such that for all
t>0
,
\rm{Cov}(\mathbf{G})=\rm{Cov}(\mathbf{X})
then we obtain that there exists universal constants
C_{2},\tilde{C}_{2},\tilde{C}_{2}^{\prime}>\infty
such that for all
\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{p}

P\big(\big|\sum_{i\leq n}(\mathbf{G}_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2}-\mathbb{E}((\mathbf{G}_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2})\big|\geq t\big)\leq 2e^{-\frac{C_{2}t}{S\|\beta\|_{2}^{2}/p}\min\Big(\frac{t\tilde{C}_{2}}{n\|\beta\|_{2}^{2}/p},\tilde{C}_{2}^{\prime}\Big)}.

Proof
Firstly we remark that we can assume without loss of generality that there exists an
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\sum_{j\leq M}c_{i,j}Z_{j}
for all
i\leq n
.
We define
F_{m}:=\sum_{\ell}\beta_{\ell}Z_{m,\ell}
and remark that the process
(F_{m})
is still a sequence of independent random variables that are
K\|\beta\|^{2}/p
subGaussian. Moreover we remark that for all
i\leq n
we have

\beta^{T}\mathbf{X}_{i}=\sum_{m\leq M}c_{i,m}F_{m}.

Define
C_{M}:=(c_{i,j})_{i\leq n,j\leq M}
then we immediately note that

\sum_{i\leq n}(\beta^{T}\mathbf{X}_{i})^{2}=F_{m}^{T}(C_{M}^{T}C_{M})F_{m}.

Hence using Theorem 1.1 of
Rudelson and Vershynin (2013)
we remark that there exists
C>0
such that

P\Big(\big|\sum_{i\leq n}(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2}-\mathbb{E}((\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\beta)^{2})\big|\geq t\Big)\leq 2e^{-\frac{tC}{K\|\beta\|^{2}/p}\min\Big(\frac{t}{{K\|\beta\|^{2}}/{p}\|C_{M}^{T}C_{M}\|_{HS}^{2}},\frac{1}{\|C_{M}^{T}C_{M}\|}\Big)}.

We note that

(C_{M}^{T}C_{M})_{m_{1},m_{2}}=\sum_{i\leq n}c_{i,m_{1}}c_{j,m_{2}}.

Hence we remark that

\displaystyle\|C_{M}^{T}C_{M}\|_{HS}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}\leq M}\big(\sum_{i}c_{i,m_{1}}c_{i,m_{2}}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\leq n}(\sum_{m}c_{i,m}c_{j,m})^{2}

We observe that if we define
\nu:=(1,\dots,1)^{T}
then we have
\rm{cov}(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\nu,\mathbf{X}_{j}^{T}\nu)=\sum_{m}c_{i,m}c_{j,m}\nu^{T}\Sigma\nu
. As we assumed
\lambda_{min}(\Sigma)>\underline{c}
we obtain that
\nu^{T}\Sigma\nu\geq\underline{c}.
This implies that

\|C_{M}^{T}C_{M}\|_{HS}^{2}\leq(\underline{c})^{-2}\sum_{i,j\leq n}\rm{Cov}(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\nu,\mathbf{X}_{j}^{T}\nu)^{2}.

To further bound this, we note that according to Lemma 26 of
Austern and Orbanz (2022)
we know that

|\rm{cov}(\nu^{T}\mathbf{X}_{i},\nu^{T}\mathbf{X}_{j})|\leq 4{\beta(i-j)^{\frac{\epsilon}{2+\epsilon}}}\max_{i}\|\nu^{T}\mathbf{X}_{i}\|_{2+\epsilon}^{2},

which directly implies, coupled with the fact that
\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\nu
is a
K_{\mathbf{X}}
subgaussian random variable, that there exists a constant
\tilde{C}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|C_{M}^{T}C_{M}\|_{HS}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 16(\underline{c})^{-2}\sum_{i,j\leq n}\rm{Cov}(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\nu,\mathbf{X}_{j}^{T}\nu)^{2}{\beta(i-j)^{\frac{2\epsilon}{2+\epsilon}}}\max_{i}\|\nu^{T}\mathbf{X}_{i}\|_{2+\epsilon}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{C}n(\underline{c})^{-2}\mathcal{S}

Similarly we obtain that there exists a constant
\tilde{C}^{\prime}
such that
